"Practitioner" means the yogin, and it is connected with "should accomplish all purposes." The meaning of āli and kāli is given in the passage beginning "Āli"; it is easy to understand.

To show that all the aims of sentient beings without exception are also accomplished through the skillful means and wisdom of the deity's form, the passage beginning "Sentient beings" is stated.

Because nirvāṇa too is accomplished through skillful means and wisdom, the passage beginning "Outside" is stated. "Outside" means gone beyond, because it has gone beyond and is separated from skillful means and wisdom. Again, "outside" indicates the way of the Perfections. The meaning is that, apart from the skillful means and wisdom of the deity's meditative absorption, there is nothing whatever that carries one across to the far shore of saṃsāra.

For that very reason, the passage beginning "Deluded" is stated. "Deluded" is one who has a mind directed solely outward. "Its pledge": because it accords with that, it is a pledge, namely the outer path. Alternatively, "a" is a negation: it is not taught to such a person. If one asks what is not taught: the pledge, namely the meditative absorption of the deity of skillful means and wisdom.

For that very reason, the passage beginning "Secret" is stated; it is easy to understand.

In "Whoever has come to know the scripture," "has come to know" means wishing to know and being capable, that is, being devoted to the inner path. "Through that he is renowned as a yogin" extends as far as "able to apply himself to suchness, and a holder of accomplishments."

For that very reason, the passage beginning "Like crystal" is stated. "Pure like crystal" means endowed with the faith of clarity and of conviction. "Its Dharma" is the path of accomplishments such as liberation, that is, deity yoga.

To show the fault in acting otherwise, the passage beginning "If" is stated. "Doubt" is being of two minds about the path taught by the guru. If one asks what happens then, the passage beginning "In an instant" is stated; it is easy to understand.

To prohibit teaching the Dharma to those who seek only desire, the passage beginning "Delighting in desire" is stated.

To show that it is to be given to those who were formerly in the way of the Perfection Vehicle, or who are deluded, and who make the welfare of beings their chief concern, it is stated:

"For the sake of benefiting sentient beings,
striving intensely, one apprehends that itself."

"Should be taught" is to be supplied. "That itself" is the selflessness of phenomena, the aspect of emptiness. "Apprehends" means understands.

To teach the guarding of the yogins' conduct with sense objects, "If" is stated. "If" means: if the yogin who strives for the welfare of others is one with attachment. "Delighting in desire" means wishing to enjoy the objects of desire. This is to be construed with "then there is one characteristic of skillful means." "Skillful means" is the path. "One" means supreme, that is, without equal. If one asks how, the passage beginning "Self-delight" is stated. "Self-delight" is the woman as object. "Moment" means occasion. Of what? Of "genuine arising," that is, when an excellent object is found.

To show the actual skillful means, the passage beginning "Vajra and bell" is stated. The union of vajra and bell is the union of vajra and lotus. If one asks how that is accomplished, it is the passage beginning "In the manner of a moment." "The manner of a moment" means being completed in a mere instant. For that very reason it is stated:

"In the yoga of oneself, declare 'I.'"

"The yoga of oneself" is the meditative absorption of Vajrasattva. "Declaring 'I'" is generating the pride of the deity.

"Abiding in the middle within the secret,
the seed of āli should be taken"
calls to mind setting the seed syllable ā at the opening of the channel of Khāgamukha.

In "Oneself, the yoga of the moment, should recite with effort what is called hūṃ," "moment" is the vajra, because it is not stable. "What is called hūṃ" is the seed syllable hūṃ. "Yoga" means joining, that is, meditating there. "Should recite with effort" calls to mind the recitation of both. By these, the union of skillful means and wisdom is taught in summary.

"Whatever little there is of body and speech should be dissolved into the union of the deity" is a prohibition of outer activities for the yogin. "Body and speech" are the actions of body and speech, such as reciting sūtras and prostrating to stūpas. "Little" means gathering them all without remainder. "The yoga of the deity" is the action of the mind, the meditative absorption of the deity. "Should be dissolved" is the prohibition of outer activities for one who meditates on the meditative absorption of the deity for a long time and with reverence.

Now, to make clear the greatness of the Dharma thus taught, the passage beginning "Palms joined" is stated. "Yogins, yoginīs and lords" is to be construed thus: the lords of yogins such as Vajrapāṇi, and the yoginīs, that is, the ḍākinīs, having listened to and heard the Dharma thus spoken by the Blessed One, joined their palms and praised him. If one asks with what they praised him, "delighted, with satisfied heart" is stated, meaning they made the heart of the Blessed One delighted and satisfied. With what? In the passage beginning "How wonderful!", "Today beings have attained liberation" means that it is most wondrous that, through those methods of meditative absorption, the vajra, the bell and so on, which are the Blessed One's speech, beings attain liberation today, that is, in this very life. "Beings" is to be taken without restriction as to caste and the like.

In "How wonderful, the path of the supreme Dharma!", "Dharma" is emptiness by intrinsic nature. "Supreme" means excellent, that is, the deity's body. "How wonderful" expresses amazement. "How wonderful, the cutter of saṃsāra!" means liberation from saṃsāra. "How wonderful, the place of yoga's liberation!" means applying oneself to the path of liberation. "How wonderful, the marvelous path of yoga!" is said because it goes to the city of great bliss.

To show that through that path accomplishment comes without hardship, "Without counting of dates" is stated; it is easy to understand.

Again, to teach the ritual procedure for the rites, the passage beginning "The wheel of rites" is stated. "The wheel of rites" is the collection of rites, and it is construed with "shall be explained." If one asks why, "the various lights of the practitioners" is stated, meaning by force of the various inclinations of the practitioners. That very thing is stated in the passage beginning "The moon maṇḍala"; it is easy to understand.

The recitation mantra is given in the passage beginning "The syllables doubled." Although this mantra belongs to the ḍākinī, the Fierce Lady, because it suits the object to be accomplished that is described below, it should be recited as Vetālī herself.

The practice is given in the passage beginning "Preta's clothing." The word "and so on" includes a hero's cloth, the covering of a newborn and the like. The rest is easy to understand. "The center of the ground" means in the center of a charnel ground. "Ordinary fire" is fire that comes from a butcher's house. In "in the center of the blazing fire," "one should perform the fire offering" is to be supplied. "The five milky ones" are five kinds of trees, such as the aśvattha. In "Honey smeared with blood," "honey" is what comes from human bone, the great black ghee. Thus, one performs the fire offering with the great black ghee and the great flesh smeared with blood. "The root mantra" is the mantra of the fire offering. "All should be done as taught before" means it should be done according to the ritual procedure for the fire offering explained earlier.

The signs are given in the passage beginning "First." In "On the fifth, the companion of the practice, having descended in the manner of a lion," the goddess Siṃhinī enters the companion of the practice, whose nature then changes, so that he appears with the form and voice of a lion. "Please take as your own, just as you wish, the dwelling places and the seat" means "Sit before me in your own form." "Then, having shown the goddess in person" means that, emerging from the disciple's body, she shows herself in the goddess's own form. "What is to be accomplished?" extends as far as "What purpose is to be accomplished?" "By the hero" means fortified by one's own mantra and mudrā, that is, one should not be afraid. "The preta's going" means going counterclockwise. The rest is easy to understand, and so it is not explained.

"Vyāghrī, Jambukī and Ulūkī are to be accomplished by that very rite" means that they are to be accomplished by the sādhana of Siṃhinī. However, the mantra of Vyāghrī is oṃ hmraṃ svāhā. That of Jambukī is oṃ ymraṃ svāhā. That of Ulūkī is oṃ kṣmraṃ svāhā.

Now, to show the conclusion of the text in a way that calls to mind that all accomplishments are achieved through the meditative absorption of skillful means and wisdom, the passage beginning "Vajra and bell" is stated; it is easy to understand.

In the passage beginning "Of Union," "chapter" is so called because this text makes clear the union of skillful means and wisdom as nondual, the purification of the enclosure and so on, the urging of Khāgamukha by skillful means and wisdom in the form of deities, the bringing under control through the ritual of binding and the application of medicines, the praise that follows meditation, and the collection of rites of the sādhanas of Siṃhinī and the others. Because it is the basis for the application of medicines, mantras and the like, it is "the Seat of Union." "Complete" means brought to its end.

Thus ends the chapter on the Seat of Union in the commentary on the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha called Smṛtinibandha.

Now, to teach the Secret Seat, the passage beginning "Masters" is stated. "Masters are to be taken" means that, in order to learn the means of accomplishment, they are to be taken up and known; it is connected with "Listen!" If one asks what kind of masters, "endowed with mantra, mudrā, union and so on" is stated. "Mantra" means the mantras for pacifying and so on. "Mudrā" means the mudrās of body and so on. Among these, the mudrā of body is the mudrā of the hands. The mudrā of speech is that indicated by verbal activity, that is, signs made with speech. The mudrā of mind is that indicated by meditation: whatever aspect of the deity, such as color, shape and faces, the mind brings to mind, that is the mudrā of mind. "Union" means meditative absorption. The word "and so on" includes the suchness of the maṇḍala and the like; "endowed" means endowed with these.

In "Hear from Vajra the characteristics of one who liberates from saṃsāra by the path as it is," "path" is so called because by it one apprehends and comes to know; that is, the treatise. "As it is" means according to its proper sequence. Because by this one liberates from saṃsāra, it is "one who liberates from saṃsāra," that is, the master. "Characteristics" means his nature.

Vajrapāṇi asked again, in the passage beginning "The master." "With what qualities" is connected with "should he be regarded." "What is meant by 'guru'?" is like asking, "What is the meaning of the term 'guru'?" "How should one apply the master?" means, "What are the characteristics of one fit to be a master?" "How should he be regarded?" means how one should apprehend him.

The answer is given in the passage beginning "All." "All" means knowledge of all, knowledge of the path, and knowledge of all aspects; because by this one knows and indicates the characteristics of these, it is the "characteristic," which is whatever the master's name is. That, moreover, is the empowerment with the name of a tathāgata. "Awakening" is what is to be known, and its nature is suchness.

In "His body is all the buddhas," because he is the nature of all the buddhas, "all the buddhas" is Vajradhara. "Body" is the body of the vajra master, and one should regard it as having that nature. "His parts abide as the limbs of awakening" means that all the parts of his body, however many there are, are the nature of the seven limbs of awakening. "His crown ornament is the five families themselves" means that the five families, Akṣobhya and the rest, in which the hundred families are summarized, are his crown ornament. "The hairs of his body are arhats" means that his countless body hairs are the nature of the noble arhats, numbering a hundred thousand koṭis. In "The worldly ones are the seat of his feet," "the worldly ones" are the worldly gods, Brahmā and the rest. "The seat of his feet" is beneath his feet, that is, as far as the soles of his feet. In "His rays are yakṣas, the secret ones and so on," "yakṣas" is simply the well-known term. "The secret ones" are the gandharvas. The word "and so on" includes the bhūtas and the like.

In "Thus the one endowed with yoga should always
regard him as a body endowed with qualities,"
"the one endowed with yoga" is the master.

To give the answer to "What kind of qualities has the master?", the passage beginning "One with wisdom" is spoken. "One with wisdom" is the master. "Characteristics" means the nature described below. If one asks what that is, the passage beginning "Thoroughly" is stated. Alternatively, "What kind of qualities" and "How should he be regarded?" have already been explained by the passage up to "Thus a body endowed with qualities." Here the answer is given to "How should one apply the master?"

"Thoroughly disciplined" means having the senses disciplined. "Of peaceful form" means having abandoned worldly attire. In "Skilled in the application of mantra and tantra," "mantra" means the mantras for pacifying and so on. "Tantra" means the wheels for pacifying and so on. Their application has the characteristics of recitation, drawing and the like, and "skilled" means in performing them. In "Knowing yoga, crafts and treatises," "yoga" means the varieties of wind and so on. "Crafts" are activities such as drawing. "Treatises" are the treatises on medicine, grammar and the like; one who knows yoga and the rest is so called. "Speaks pleasantly everywhere" means speaks gently, and it is construed with "to all sentient beings." "Regards all sentient beings as his children" means cherishing them all, that is, loving-kindness. "Always delights in generosity and the rest" means being endowed with generosity, moral discipline, patience and the rest. In "Devoted to yoga and meditative concentration," "yoga" is the union of skillful means and wisdom, and "meditative concentration" is the meditative absorption of the deity. The passage beginning "Compassion" is easy to understand.

"Since he has generated a mind of equality" means being impartial toward enemies, friends and those in between.

In "One who fully knows the ten principles," the ten principles are the maṇḍala, one's own meditative absorption, the mudrā, the karaṇa, the posture, mantra recitation, the fire offering, worship, the application of rites, and the concluding withdrawal.

In "He is declared to be called 'master,'" "he" refers to the qualities from "thoroughly disciplined" up to "the ten principles." Because those who seek what is good should cultivate them, they are qualities, and "he" is one endowed with them. "Is declared to be called 'master'" means he is declared to be a master. Therefore he is also declared by the term "guru," since he is moved and engaged by those qualities. As it is also said:

"Whether it be oneself or another,
because he speaks just as it was taught,
the wise everywhere declare him
to be master and guru."

Now, to show the conduct of the disciple, the passage beginning "Furthermore" is stated. What the disciple practices is the conduct of the disciple. That very thing is stated in the passage beginning "Yoginī." "The yoginī and the deity of union" is connected with "One should not disparage the master, the secret." Here "yoginī" means the wisdom ḍākinī and the others. "The deity of union" is the deity endowed with skillful means and wisdom, that is, the master who has that nature.

In "And the very deity who practices the pledges," the pledges are the five nectars and the five hooks. Because he enjoys and makes manifest by practicing them, he is "the deity who practices the pledges," that is, the master.

"Endowed with the secret of the torma and so on" means the suchness of the torma, the fire offering and so on; one endowed with these is the master.

"The secret" is the great Vajradhara, and one should not disparage the master who has that nature. This too has been said: "The awakening mind and the master are nondual and cannot be made distinct." Therefore the master himself is Vajradhara.

"If one disparages him, one quickly goes to Avīci" means immediately upon death, without any interval.

In some versions of the text it reads:

"The yoginī and the deity of union,
the one who practices the pledges of the deity,
the yoga, the secret, the torma and the rest,
and the master, the secret, should not be disparaged."

There it is to be construed thus: not only for the master alone, but one who disparages the yoginīs and the others also falls into Avīci.

"And being utterly without the pledges" means turning one's back, out of aversion, on the pledges as they have been obtained; this too is to be construed with the preceding line, "one quickly goes to Avīci."

In the passage beginning "Seat," "seat" is the seat on which he sits, such as a platform. "Bed" and "shoes" are simply to be understood. If one asks what about them, it is construed with "one should not step over or trample them"; it is easy to understand.

"Umbrella, clothing and articles of use" are the articles used by the guru, such as his umbrella and clothing; they are construed with "If one uses them oneself, one goes to Avīci."

In "One should not step over or trample his shadow," the shadow is the master's shadow; this means walking on the shadow and jumping over the shadow. "Trampling" means pressing it underfoot.

In "One should not speak words of contradiction or harassment," "words of contradiction" are mocking the master's faults, even merely in jest, and, when the master gives a command, turning it back with words such as "There is none" or "I cannot." "Words of harassment" are insistently pressing one's own tasks on the master when it is not convenient for him. One should not speak such words. All of these too are to be construed with the result stated before: "one quickly goes to Avīci."

If one asks what fault arises in this life, the passage beginning "If one transgresses" is stated. "Harm" means meeting with suffering. To show that very thing, "One's aims decline and one becomes far from glory" is stated. "Glory" is characterized as the accumulations of merit and wisdom. "Aims" are their results. The rest is easy to understand.

If one asks why all this is so, it is stated:

"Because union of mantra, mudrā and the rest
is accomplished by his path."

"By his path" means by the means taught by the guru.

"Preceded by giving the precepts, one then performs the maṇḍala and the rest" means that a master endowed with the characteristics taught before should first give the precepts of the ten virtues, and, preceded by going for refuge to the Three, should bring a disciple with the characteristics taught before into the maṇḍala and so on, and then confer empowerment.

The crown empowerment is given:

"With the single syllable taught before
the five buddhas are to be indicated."

"The syllable taught before" is the syllable hūṃ. "Single" is the syllable a. Here oṃ svāhā should also be understood, so that it becomes the mantra oṃ hūṃ a svāhā. "The five buddhas" is the empowerment of the diadem, which has the nature of the five buddhas; it is to be given with that mantra.

In the passage beginning "Its yoga," "its yoga" is the yoga of the crown empowerment. "As desired" means whatever one desires and in whatever way one desires.

"One who possesses the mantra empowerment
should meditate on the eight syllables"
means that after the mantra empowerment one sets the eight syllables in eight places of the body.

"One should know the dharma
of nondual torma-giving and empowerment"
teaches the empowerment of vajra and bell, preceded by giving the torma.

"To one who possesses the empowerment of union, primordial wisdom and consciousness should be taught" means that to one who possesses those empowerments, primordial wisdom and the rest should be taught. Then, after the name empowerment, the master empowerment should be given. For that very reason it is stated:

"Having conferred the master empowerment,
all, the secret and the rest, are taught."

The word "all" indicates, in the master empowerment, the embrace with the hands that hold the vajra and the vajra bell.

"The secret" means the secret empowerment that follows it. By the words "and the rest," the fourth empowerment is included. This is the sequence of the empowerments. To teach all of it, the passage beginning "First" is stated: having completed the maṇḍala, one should first carry out the preparatory rite (adhivāsana) for the disciple. The passage beginning "Having draped the upper robe over one shoulder" describes what the disciple does. To the disciple thus standing in front, in order to purify the disciple's mouth, one gives a tooth stick and water over which the mantra oṃ a ji hva saṃ barta ni hūṃ phaṭ has been recited seven times, and the vow should then be conferred. If one asks by what kind of master it should be conferred, the passage beginning "The master" is stated: the master, seated on a lion throne and meditating on himself as his own deity, should confer it. If one asks what is done when it is conferred, the passage beginning "With the hand" is stated: the disciple joins the palms at the heart, the master takes hold of them with his right hand, and so confers it. If one asks what the actual vow is, the passage beginning "The Buddha" is stated: from "In the Buddha, the Dharma, and the Saṅgha" up to "I shall keep this moral discipline," the disciple should recite it. If one asks how it should be recited, it is stated:

"Thus twice and three times
one with a mind steady in evenness recites."

That is, with body and the rest held even and completely pure, it should be recited three times.

Then, preceded by teaching the profound Dharma such as the Perfection of Wisdom, the disciple should be led into the maṇḍala; this is the preparatory rite. Then, having worshiped the maṇḍala with the twenty offerings, ringing the vajra and bell and praising with song, one covers the disciple's face with silk, has him hold a flower in his cupped hands, and places him in front of the maṇḍala. One confers the vow once more and recites the three purifications beginning oṃ sva bhā va. Then again, with the passage beginning

"Pride and anger,
coarseness and harsh words are to be abandoned,"

one establishes him in loving-kindness and the rest. With the three mantra phrases beginning oṃ pañca ḍā ki nī one has the disciple prostrate three times, and after reciting the mantras beginning sa ma ya stvaṃ sa ma ya ho, in the manner of the passage beginning "a is the supreme of all syllables," one blesses body, speech, and mind and gives the five nectars. Then the descent of wisdom should be performed. With the mantra tiṣṭha vajra sa ma ya ma nu pā la ya hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ ho ho ho svā hā the flower is cast, and with oṃ jñā na cakṣu hūṃ svā hā the face covering is removed and the maṇḍala should be shown. Then the disciple stands at the eastern gate with palms joined at the heart and is made to say oṃ vajra maṇḍala mahā maṇḍala pra bisto haṃ yo ga maṇḍala mahā maṇḍala pra darśa no hi guhya maṇḍala mahā maṇḍala dha kṣi pto haṃ sa ma yo haṃ. The master arranges the armor on the disciple's limbs, has the disciple take on the pride of the deity, and should then confer the water empowerment and the rest.

In this context, the passage beginning "The actions of the body are three" teaches the explanation of the Dharma to the disciple.

"By restraining the body, the samaya is guarded"

means that by thoroughly restraining the actions of body and the rest, one guards the samayas that are to be guarded. Moreover, the word samaya teaches that one upholds, and does not transgress, the samayas that are to be eaten, which are characterized as the five hooks and the five nectars. Then, in order to explain the profound and vast Dharma to one whose body and the rest are held in evenness, the passage beginning "The mind" is stated. To lead the disciple into the maṇḍala, the covering of the face is taught:

"Then, having covered the eyes with cloth,
one should lead him into the place of the torma."

Here "the place of the torma" is the ground of the maṇḍala.

"Having taken his hand with one's hand,
one should again teach the undertaking."

Here "again" means that, having led him to the door of the maṇḍala, one confers the vow once more; it refers back to the vow taught earlier. The vow is stated in the passage beginning "The Buddha": here too one recites the vows mentioned before, and after that this seed syllable is to be recited. "The Seat of Union" is the maṇḍala.

After the wisdom-gnosis empowerment, in order to teach the fourth, the passage beginning "Supreme realization of the ultimate" is stated. "The ultimate" is ultimate gnosis. Because it is to be realized, it is "realization." Because it is preeminent, it is "supreme." Because it is like a lion, it is "lion," meaning that which is to be accomplished. "Having recognized the disciples" means having recognized that the disciple is devoted to the profound nature of the ultimate; it is to be construed with "the king of reality should be taught to him." If one asks further what that disciple is like, it is stated:

"Because one endowed with yoga tears the woven cloth of the obscuring veil."

"One endowed with yoga" means one who delights in yoga. If one asks for what purpose, it is "in order to tear the woven cloth of the obscuring veil," that is, in order to cut through delusion. If one asks what kind of king of reality is taught, the passage beginning "The faults of existence" is stated. Existence is the five aggregates; because they are suffering, they are the fault. "Producing" refers to the ground from which suffering arises, namely the afflictions such as attachment. "Having destroyed" means by abandoning them. If one asks what one does having destroyed them, it is stated:

"Accomplishing the ultimate yoga."

The ultimate yoga is the accomplishment of the Great Seal (Mahāmudrā), and this is what accomplishes it. "The king of reality" is nondual gnosis: because it shines clearly without becoming other than as the guru taught it, it is the king of reality. The passage beginning "a" is easy to understand. The passage beginning

"To one endowed with the five nectars"

concerns giving the five nectars. The passage beginning

"At the feet of the guru"

concerns prostration. The passage beginning

"Endowed with the vajra mudrā"

concerns arranging armor on the body; the meaning of the words is easy to understand. The passage beginning "Furthermore" is the prediction. "Perfect awakening" means great awakening. "Of all phenomena" is to be construed as "the most sublime and chief of all." The passage beginning "Then" is easy to understand.

To teach that the trainings are also given at the end of the empowerment, the passage beginning "Phenomena" is stated.

"Phenomena are by intrinsic nature pure"

means holding them to be of a completely pure essential nature, because they are free from the intrinsic nature of the apprehended object and the rest. If one asks what this leads to, the passage beginning "The very intrinsic nature of things" is stated, because it amounts to clinging to emptiness. To counter that, it is stated:

"In the absence of an intrinsic nature of things,
the stages of perfect awakening are taught."

Here awakening is the realization of reality. Absence of intrinsic nature is absence of essential nature: ultimately, the three, namely the one who realizes, what is to be realized, and the realization, are not apprehended. Since they are not apprehended, who teaches what to whom? It is a teaching in which there is nothing to teach. This shows that, abiding in the understanding of selflessness, one turns away from non-virtue and engages in virtue.

That very point is stated in the passage beginning "First." "The Dharma" means the virtues. "First" means what goes first, that is, the root. If one asks what that is, "patience" is stated. It is shown by

"One should not torment sentient beings."

In the other sense, however, one should not be patient toward one's own afflictions.

"One should not speak harsh words to others":

words that become hurtful by exposing the faults of others are harsh words. Since speaking them displeases everyone and gives rise to pain, one should turn away from them. As an exception, however, exposing the faults and failings of oneself is excluded from this.

Moral discipline is stated in the passage beginning "Moral discipline," which is easy to understand. In the other sense, however, the view of a self, such as grasping at sentient beings, is to be slain.

The abandoning of taking what is not given is stated in the passage beginning "The blazing fire." Here too, in the other sense, one should not take the substance of the awakening mind from the lotus of wisdom without its being given.

The abandoning of lying is stated in the passage beginning "Gross and." "Gross" things are jewels and the like; "subtle" things are dust and the like. If one asks how it is guarded, it is stated:

"For the yogin who practices yoga,
there is no lying."

Yoga is samādhi. Practice is cultivation in thorough meditative equipoise. Whenever one is constantly in thorough meditative equipoise, lying is to be guarded against, because lying arises from not being in equipoise. Here too, teaching that all phenomena are false is excepted.

As for the ground of all accomplishments, to teach that it is complete restraint in every respect and the teaching of sentient beings, the passage beginning

"Conduct well restrained, and"

is stated. To teach the giving of encouragement, the passage beginning

"Whoever, all-knowing, to the disciple"

is stated. The rest is easy to understand.

Since giving with a lowly aim is the chief obstacle to liberation, to refute it the passage beginning "Sentient beings" is stated. "Existence" means focusing on enjoyments, as on a field. "By that very means one trades" means seeking after wealth. "The sprout grows" refers to the sprout of the tree of enjoyments produced by the seed of giving. If one asks what this leads to, it is stated:

"The fruit for beings blocks the path."

"Beings" are the six classes of beings. "The fruit" is obtaining enjoyments there. Since it is said, "Through giving, even in the lower realms one obtains enjoyments," through giving one obtains enjoyments even in the states of the lower realms. "Blocks the path" means that this fruit comes to block the path to buddhahood, because one becomes attached to enjoyments and because transgressions arise on that basis. As an exception, however, dedication to great awakening that is endowed with non-apprehension is excluded. To show this, the passage beginning "Heaven" is stated. "The embodied one" means consciousness. "Made" means made to possess enjoyments. "Through enjoyments of delight and play" means by indulging in them again and again under the power of ignorance. "One falls into existence" means being born in states without leisure.

So that yogins devoted to meditation may give up activities such as making stūpas, the passage beginning "Stūpas" was taught.

"One does not bind the mudrās of the hands"

means here that one binds the mudrā of the union of embrace. In the other case, these are not to be prohibited for those who have no attachment to yoga.

"Treatises are easy to obtain;
their application is hard to find."

Here "treatises" means listening to treatises, reading them, and the like. They are "easy to obtain" because everyone follows that course. Yet by it one cannot accomplish the result, as it is said:

"Without heeding the physician's words, how could a patient
who needs treatment be cured by medicines?
By merely reading the medical treatise,
is the patient helped by that?"

Alternatively, "easy to obtain" means that listening and reading are easy to take up, as in the saying, "Here there is no one who is not learned." "Their application" means direct realization preceded by the three kinds of wisdom. It is "hard to find" because it is hard to put into practice and does not become directly perceived through mere hearing, reading, and the like, since it arises from practice with great effort. Others say that "treatises" are outer treatises and "application" is the scripture of the great yoga.

"By the path of the masters
the wise should look."

Here "the path of the masters" means listening and the rest, which are easy to obtain. It teaches this: what is to be seen by practicing oneself, with that as a preliminary, is not made directly perceived by mere hearing and reading. "Should look" means that the application should be seen through practicing respectfully for a long time, and for that very reason the word "wise" is stated. That too is as it is said:

"The tathāgatas are those who show the way;
you yourselves must do it."

To teach that what is to be habituated is also accomplished from one's own mind, it is stated:

"Of one's own deity and other deities,
one's own deity is the supreme deity."

"One's own deity" means making oneself the deity.

"Other deities" are deities that are cast, hammered, and the like. Just as an outer deity is fashioned into a deity from outer substances by outer activity, so one's own deity is made from the mind alone by inner mental attention. If one asks which of these two accumulates merit here, it is stated:

"One's own deity is the supreme deity."

The intended meaning is this: how could the two, the gnosis that is made into the body of the deity from one's own mind, and the inert outer deity of the deluded who grasp at a self, be alike in producing merit? They are like the ocean and the water in an ox's hoofprint, for through the distinction of the cause the result becomes distinguished.

If one asks, "If that is so, what is to be done about those others?", it is stated:

"Since they cannot do so in their own form,
they should paint images and the like."

The intended meaning is that those who cling to outer things rely on outer deities, and in this way they create merit in keeping with that; there is no contradiction.

In the passage beginning "The Secret Seat," the word "treatise" (prakaraṇa) means that this text is a prakaraṇa because it clarifies the characteristics of the master, the activities of the disciple, the empowerment of the disciple, and the rest.

This is the first section, on the Secret Seat, from the commentary on the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha called Smṛtinibandha.

To teach the section on the complete compendium of the means of accomplishment, the passage beginning "There" is spoken. "Application" is the instruction on reality. "There" means in the treatises; that is, it is hard to find in the treatises. "Vajra king, the chief" is an address to Vajrapāṇi. "Vajra" refers to the secret yakṣas; because he has become their lord and delights them, he is the chief king. When one wonders what fault would follow if one does not know the instruction on reality, it is stated:

"Mantrins who lack reality,
who increase existence, fall below."

"Fall below" means that they wander and remain in saṃsāra. For that very reason "who increase existence" is stated.

Understanding that the Blessed One's words had opened the occasion, Vajrapāṇi made his request, in the passage beginning

"Since wonder has arisen with regard to the body."

"The Dharma of reality" means the Dharma of the reality of the body, or the reality of the Dharma of the body. That very summary is set out further in the passage beginning "Of yoga." Yoga is the samādhi of reality. "Measure" means intrinsic nature.

"What are mantra and mudrā like?"

means "What is the intrinsic nature of mantra and mudrā like?" "The measure of the body" is the intrinsic nature of the body of the elements.

"What is the measure of the full application
of the yogin's torma rite?"

refers to the nature of rasayana, the practice of the elixir.

"That by which beings are accomplished,
how are its characteristic and measure?"

Here "that by which" is the cause. "Beings" are the beings such as gods. The meaning is: what is the characteristic of the cause of beings, and what is the measure of beings, that is, in what does their essential nature finally consist?

The answer is given in the passage beginning "What is to be indicated." "What is to be indicated" means what is to be known, that is, the body as it is commonly known. "The measure of the mind" is the intrinsic nature of the mind; this continues up to "The body is the intrinsic nature of the mind," the intrinsic nature of the mind of the body.

To teach the samādhi of a mind without an object of focus, and in order to turn away activity accompanied by signs, the passage beginning "In my own elements" is stated.

"Since my own elements are not stable"

means that the elements of one's own body, earth, water, fire, and wind, are impermanent. A samādhi of that kind is a samādhi that grasps at a self, because it focuses on signs and focuses on what changes.

"The torma rite and recitation,
maṇḍala, the rites of fire and water,
mudrā, and offerings of incense and the rest:
even the yogin who obtains their fruit
will fall into the ocean of existence."

This teaches that even the fruit of virtue accompanied by signs is unstable, because, having enjoyed for a short time the heavenly fruit of the torma and the rest, one again declines from it and falls. Here "recitation" is mantra recitation. "Maṇḍala" is the samādhi of the maṇḍala of deities. "Fire" is the fire offering in fire. "The rite of water" is the fire offering in water. "Mudrā" is binding the mudrās of the hands. "Offerings of incense and the rest" are outer offerings such as flowers, incense, scents, lamps, and food for the deities. The intended meaning is that all of these are impermanent. "There is no obtaining of the fruit" is intended to mean that if a yogin engages in these and the like with clinging, he then falls into the ocean of existence. Through clinging to the torma rite and the rest he obtains the fruit of saṃsāra, because, having enjoyed the fruit of heaven for a short time, he declines and falls.

Taking up "the ocean of existence" itself, and in order to request a full answer to that very question, "That by which beings are accomplished, how are its characteristic and measure?", the passage beginning "Existence" is stated.

"The distinct arisings of existence"

means taking birth separately in the desire, form, and formless realms.

"That by which is their measure"

means that whatever births are taken, "their" measure is the intrinsic nature of the mind; they are taken by the mind.

"How should the mind be viewed?"

means "By what kind of mind are what kinds of bodies taken?"

The answer is given in the passage beginning "Saṃsāra."

"Saṃsāra, about which you have asked"

means "Listen to what you have asked about: the mind's taking up of existence." If one asks by what kind of mind, it is stated:

"The mind, having washed away doubt,"

that is, by a mind free from the stain of doubt. Alternatively, doubt means the many movements of the mind; it is by being free of them and resting in meditative equipoise.

In the passage beginning "Self-willed gnosis," the word "self-willed" is connected with "comes to be accomplished": since it is accomplished under the power of one's own deeds, karma, and afflictions, it is "self-willed." If one asks what is accomplished, it is gnosis without entity: the very mind that is pure by intrinsic nature comes to be accomplished as those various states. Alternatively, "self-willed gnosis" is self-aware gnosis. If one asks what it is like, it is stated:

"As the sky-going wind moves."

That is, one takes birth just as wind moves through the sky. Alternatively, "the sky-going wind is produced" refers to the time when the father's seed is emitted, that is, to that moment. In some versions of the text it reads:

"By being bound by the sky-going wind."

In that case the sky-goers are karma and the afflictions, because they move without a body. "Wind" is self-explanatory. "Bound" means that the body of gnosis is bound by them, and by this, birth is accomplished.

"The formless" are the gods of the sphere of infinite space, the sphere of infinite consciousness, the sphere of nothingness, and the sphere of neither existence nor nonexistence. "Form" is the form realm, namely the gods of Brahmakāyika, Brahmapurohita, and Mahābrahmā; of Parīttaśubha, Apramāṇaśubha, and Śubhakṛtsna; of Parīttābha, Apramāṇābha, and Ābhāsvara; of Anabhraka, Puṇyaprasava, and Bṛhatphala; and of one portion of the Pure Abodes.

"They are accomplished through focus in meditative concentration"

means that they are accomplished through the karma of entering absorption in the meditative concentrations and the rest.

"The gods of desire" are the gods of the Four Great Kings, the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, Yāma, Tuṣita, Nirmāṇarati, and Paranirmitavaśavartin. By the words "and the rest" the human destiny and the others are included: the humans of Jambudvīpa, of Pūrvavideha in the east, of Uttarakuru in the north, and of Aparagodānīya in the west; the hungry ghosts; the animals; and the hells of Saṃjīva, Kālasūtra, Saṃghāta, Raurava, Mahāraurava, Tapana, Pratāpana, and Avīci, which together with the eight cold hells are called the eight great hells. These twenty abodes are thus called "those with desire."

"They always act according to their desire"

means that, having taken a body in those states through consciousness, they experience the result just in accordance with the karma they have performed. "Thus" means taking birth in the three realms of existence. "Humans" means humans and the rest. "One's own desire and the rest are accomplished" means that, under the power of karma, consciousness takes bodies in different abodes.

"The things that liberate from that"

are the means of liberation from that saṃsāra.

"The yogin, by going together
with consciousness, accomplishes the destiny."

Here "consciousness" is the all-base consciousness. "Together" refers to the cooperating condition: the destiny is accomplished through the appropriating cause, which is karma, and the cooperating condition, which is the afflictions. When the earlier maturation is exhausted, consciousness takes on a body of maturation in one destiny after another. This is also stated in the Thirty Verses:

"The latencies of appropriation, together with
the latencies of the two kinds of grasping,
when the former maturation is exhausted,
produce another maturation."

There too, "the two kinds of grasping" are grasping as the apprehended and grasping as the apprehender. "Maturation" is the all-base consciousness.

Having thus explained the reason by which the destinies of ordinary beings are accomplished, in order to teach that of the yogin, the passage beginning "The yogin" is spoken. "The yogin" is one endowed with the samādhi of the deity in which skillful means and wisdom are joined in equality. "Consciousness" is innate gnosis, which is stainless. "By going together" means together with the deity yoga just described, that is, by meditating on it. "Accomplishes the destiny" means the destiny of one's own choosing, that is, going wherever one wishes, up to omniscience.

In order that the compiler might clarify that very path, he requests again, in the passage that begins:

"Being in doubt about the nature of mind,
O Blessed One, please explain this too."

"Please explain" means "please free me from doubt." "This too" implies something else.

In the passage beginning

"Wisdom, consciousness, the intrinsic nature of things,"

the word "wisdom" is connected with "How is gnosis?" Here it is wisdom because it knows thoroughly, and it is innate gnosis. If one asks what it thoroughly knows, it is "consciousness, the intrinsic nature of things": since it is the things themselves, form and the rest, that are to be known, it is consciousness, their intrinsic nature. If one asks how it knows, "not knowing" is stated: because it does not know them as having the nature of apprehended and apprehender, it is "not knowing." For that very reason the passage beginning "Consciousness" is stated. "bi" means the objects. Because it knows their reality, it is consciousness, that is, wisdom. Because it is individually self-aware, it is gnosis, and its characteristic is its intrinsic nature. In some versions of the text it reads:

"That by which consciousness is the entity of existence."

That is, consciousness is the entity of existence. It is to be construed as: since it always arises in duality, how should the yogin view the characteristic of nondual gnosis?

The answer is given in the passage beginning "Crystal and." "Like crystal" means pure by intrinsic nature, because it appears in many aspects. If one asks whether those aspects really exist, it is stated:

"In mind there is neither fault nor virtue."

That is, it is free of the nature of apprehended and apprehender. Alternatively, the aspects of what is discordant and of the remedy are not established as entities. If one asks how, then, it appears, it is stated:

"Through meeting with objects,
it is stained by the faults of existence."

"Meeting with objects" means appearing as dual. "Existence" is the latency of grasping at entities, because from it dual appearance comes to exist and arises. "Faults" are the defilements such as the afflictions, because these corrupt the mind and make it deluded.

In some versions of the text it reads:

"Through engaging and meeting with objects,
it is stained by the faults of existence."

Objects are form and the rest. Engaging is eye consciousness and the rest. Meeting is the eye faculty and the rest. It is connected with "it is stained by these aspects." If one asks by the power of what, "by the faults of existence" is stated, that is, by the power of the latencies of conceptual elaboration.

If one asks, "If that is so, how does nirvāṇa come about?", the passage beginning "That by which" is stated. "That by which" means the gnosis by which. "The entity of existence" is the intrinsic nature of saṃsāra. "Peace" is nirvāṇa: saṃsāra and nirvāṇa are inseparable. "Abiding in objects" is the engagement of apprehended and apprehender. To teach that very point, it is stated:

"Applied just as they abide."

Relying on the objects, form and the rest, and on the faculties, the eye and the rest, the elements of consciousness engage. This teaches the eighteen elements, which are applied as apprehended and apprehender just as object and subject appear.

"Discipline it as one does a son or a disciple"

means that it is held in the way one turns a son or a disciple away from improper places, sets them in the place of what is to be known, and keeps hold of them. By these words it is taught that the gnosis by which one turns back the clinging to the intrinsic nature of saṃsāra as apprehended and the rest, and is led into the reality of luminosity, knows the reality of saṃsāra just as it is; and so that very mind is taught to be of the essential nature of nirvāṇa. Thus it is said:

"Apart from saṃsāra itself,
there is no other nirvāṇa."

And elsewhere too:

"Whatever is the limit of saṃsāra,
that is the limit of nirvāṇa."

And the master Nāgārjuna also said:

"Saṃsāra and nirvāṇa
do not exist as two.
The thorough understanding of existence
is what is called nirvāṇa."

In some versions of the text it reads:

"Having turned back abiding in objects,
apply it to the place just as it is,
and constrain it at the door of the son."

In that case, "turning back abiding in objects" means turning back clinging to the apprehended and the rest. "Apply it to the place just as it is" means to the place of the innate. To teach that very point, "at the door of the opening" is stated: the awakening mind is to be held within the vajra jewel.

If one asks what is to be done next, the passage beginning "In its center" is stated. "Center" is the middle between attachment and freedom from attachment, that is, in the center that is neither attachment nor freedom from attachment. Alternatively, it is free from the extremes of permanence and annihilation, that is, in the center without arising and ceasing. If one asks what is to be known, "consciousness" is stated, that is, the intrinsic nature of mind. If one asks how, it is like space. Alternatively, "what" means that which is uncommon and becomes distinctive. "jñā na" means self-awareness.

To teach that very point, the passage beginning "Colorless, and" is stated: it is free of colors such as white and yellow. "Displaying all" means appearing as all colors. Alternatively, "a" is negation, because all attachments are adventitious. "Unconnected" is because nothing whatever can be joined to it. "Connected with the embodied" is because it has entered into all things. Alternatively, "unconnected" is because no phenomenon whatever can obscure it, and "connected" is because it pervades all embodied beings. "Formless" is because nothing whatever harms or injures it; alternatively, it is formless because it is unchanging. "The form of all" is because everything is bound by it.

"Likewise it is neither short nor long"

shows the distinctive features of the formless. By these words is taught the gnosis of wisdom, which knows just as it is, like water poured into water, the reality of the mind that is like crystal.

If one asks, "If that is so, how do miraculous powers and the rest come about?", the passage beginning "Of the mind" is stated. "Reality" means what is unfabricated. This teaches that innate gnosis itself manifests as miraculous powers and the rest. If one asks whether miraculous power exists in reality, "without entity" is stated, up to "ultimately, the intrinsic nature of mind." If one asks whether the reality of mind exists, "without characteristic" is stated, the intention being that where there is no characteristic, there is no entity either.

"It bestows all characteristics"

continues up to "it purifies all characteristics."

To teach the meditation of the deity's samādhi with a mind free of characteristics, it is stated:

"Through meditating without entities,
the meditations should be indicated."

"The meditations" are the samādhi of the deity. "Meditating without entities" means having grasped selflessness. "Should be indicated" means should be made habitual. To teach that very point, the passage beginning "Form and" is stated, which is easy to understand. In the passage beginning "All," "all" means the aspects of the deity. Focusing completely is focusing on selflessness. The body of yoga is the body in which emptiness and compassion are inseparable. Faultless means without afflictions.

Again it is said, in the passage beginning "From the secret": "the secret" is gnosis. "Secret" means free of the intrinsic nature of the faculties. "The great secret" means free of the intrinsic nature of objects, that is, beyond objects. "The secrets of consciousness" means beyond the intrinsic nature of consciousness, that is, gnosis. These teach that it is mere self-aware gnosis, which ultimately is free from the joining of faculties and the rest.

"Hidden from reading treatises and the like,
and far from the characteristics of objects"

means that it does not become an object of reading treatises, listening, and the like; for that very reason it is hidden. Alternatively, it is concealed within the outer treatises. If one asks from where such gnosis is to be known and received, the passage beginning "What is to be indicated" is stated: the characteristic of gnosis comes to be taught through the path of the guru.

"With mind and body alike at ease" means that both body and mind are happy, because here happiness is to be adopted and suffering is to be abandoned. For that very reason it is said:

"The body should not be tormented,
and the mind too should not be tormented."

This teaches the means of accomplishment. Another means of accomplishment is then taught, with the passage beginning "In a pleasing place." In a pleasing place, with body and mind at ease, one sits on a comfortable seat in the hero's cross-legged posture and lowers the eyes to the tip of the nose. One places the right hand upon the crossed legs and sets the left on top of it. One fills the body down to the navel with the inhaled breath, straightens the waist and lets it swell out fully. Having generated the mind of sameness with svabhāva śuddha and the rest, one makes offerings mentally with scents and so on; this is to be joined with "one should draw up the nectar." In the passage beginning "mental scent," it is the mind, pure by its very nature, and sameness alone that are expressed by the words "scent" and so on. In the passage beginning "nectar torma," the nectar itself is the torma, because it makes the body strong.

"Drawing it up from everywhere, one should consume it"

means that, having drawn it up from all the upper and lower sites of the body by the rite that comes below, one should consume it, that is, drink it.

The placing of mantras in the nine doors is taught with the passage beginning "yellow in color." In

"The bell, the letter of water,"

"the letter of the bell" is the letter hūṃ, because the tip of the foot stroke attached beneath it is called the bell. "Water" is the eyes: it is placed in the eyes. The rest is easy to understand.

The rite of drawing up the nectar is taught with the passage beginning "the upper fangs." "Blessed by entering" means blessed by the breath entering and leaving. "Like the color" is to be joined with "milky water."

"The nectar water relied on by the tongue"

is the nectar that flows from where the two fangs and the tongue meet. To show just this, "the root of the uvula" is mentioned. Alternatively, "shown by the tongue" means turning the tongue back against the palate and pressing it there. If one asks where that nectar is to be made to fall, the text says:

"It is to fall within the root of the uvula,"

that is, it falls into the cow's udder. If one asks from where it falls, the passage beginning "the forehead" is stated; these are the upper sites. The lower sites are shown below, with the passage beginning "The water enters."

In "at the door of the stūpa," "door" means the opening, that is, within the cow's udder. If one asks what this is, it is the letter hūṃ; it is a stūpa because it is the abode of the five buddhas. If one asks how the nectar is drawn up, the passage beginning

"Below, with the seed of wind"

is stated: together with the upward-moving and downward-moving winds, by the light rays of the seed hūṃ. In "like a machine," it is drawn up from all the upper and lower sites, the way oil is pressed out of sesame seeds. If one asks where it is washed by this, "of the cow's hoof" is mentioned, meaning into the heart, the hollow of the lungs. If one asks where it is placed in the end, the text says:

"From the one door, place it in the nine doors,"

meaning that from the door of the cow's hoof it is to be made to dissolve into all of them, the door of Brahmā and the rest.

To teach the yoga, the passage beginning "in the center of the heart" is stated. "The stream of milk" is the letter hūṃ.

"With all the vowel letters,
all the sites around the moon disk's faces
are to be encircled"

means encircled by the twelve vowel letters. The meaning of these lines is this: they show that at the heart, upon the letter saṃ, is a moon disk, upon it the letter hūṃ, and around it, last of all, a ring of the twelve vowel letters. In the passage beginning "the cow's hoof," the cow's hoof here runs from the center of the eyebrows into the palate. The passage beginning "form and" teaches that one meditates on Vajrasattva at the time of drawing up the nectar; this will be explained at length below.

Recitation is taught in the passage beginning "milk." The milk, the seed of the moon, is the letter hūṃ, because the stream of nectar drips from it. Put together here, it becomes oṃ hūṃ a svāhā; the a will be shown below.

To make clear the generation of Vajrasattva, the passage beginning "the root of the nectar" is stated. The root of the nectar is the letter a. "The sprout" is the body of Vajrasattva: one meditates on the body of Vajrasattva generated from a. If one asks what this leads to, the passage beginning "that and that" is stated: the result is that the body and wisdom increase.

"The site of his yogas:
steady in an empty cave and the like"

and so on: the intent is that this yoga should be practiced in such places. To make this very point clear, Vajrapāṇi asked the question beginning "How." The answer is given in the passage beginning "nondual." The meaning of these lines is: having washed the cloth of the mind with the water of the gnosis of emptiness, one dyes it with the pigment of Vajrasattva generated from the letter a. "Misdeeds and good deeds" are virtuous and sinful things.

"By which, victorious over its own nature,
it is to be illuminated by gnosis"

means it is to be made luminous with the color of wisdom. The rest is easy to understand.

The radiance of Vajrasattva's light is taught in the passage beginning "Akaniṣṭha." "Then Akaniṣṭha" means in the center of the eyebrows. The words "and so on" are to include the heart. "The seven levels below the earth" is the center of the navel, because it is encircled by seven threads. "One should meditate" means that one should make the nectar fall into these, one after another.

To teach another practice, the passage beginning "the heart" is stated. With these lines the text shows that, having meditated that everything is empty by means of the hūṃ resting on a moon at the heart, one meditates, upon a wind maṇḍala, on the wisdom ḍākinī generated on a moon from the seed hūṃ, as described before; and having meditated on the letter hūṃ on a moon at the heart, one comes to have the qualities taught before.

Another practice of Vajrasattva is taught in the passage beginning "the other." With these lines too the text shows the following. One imagines oneself as Vajrasattva resting on a moon. "Consciousness upon a lotus and a moon at the heart" means that one meditates on the letter hūṃ at the heart. One also imagines the other person as red. Light radiates from one's own heart; "his" means that it enters the heart of the one to be subjugated, and is then drawn back again and meditated on at one's own heart. "Eight" means that if one recites the mantra of the letter hūṃ eight hundred times, one will certainly come to know the mind of another.

Now, to teach the secret fire offering, the passage beginning "Then" is stated; it is for the most part easy to understand. In

"At the very center of the navel of the knot,
on its sides, seven fingers,"

"the navel of the knot" is the knot at the navel. It consists of two lines, like nooses, knotted into each other and running upward and downward; it is to be visualized at the center of the navel.

"On its sides, seven fingers"

means that at the center of the knot at the navel, it is encircled seven times by a line that comes forth from the drop of aṃ. "Fingers" is to be taken as a word for lines. "On its sides" means on the sides of the knot at the navel. Because that knot at the navel is itself the hearth, the word "hearth" is mentioned. To teach its particular features, the passage beginning "lord of nāgas" is stated; the meaning is that the lines of the knot at the center of the navel lie there like the form of a coiled serpent.

"All the letters are to be set ablaze"

means this. The letter aṃ that rests at the center of that hearth has the nature of fire. Kindled by the wind maṇḍala below, it makes the seven lines that came forth from aṃ take on the nature of blazing firelight. "All the letters" means that those seven lines are to be seen as having the nature of the vowels and consonants.

"In its center, the lotus of awareness"

means that one should meditate on a lotus above the knot at the navel.

"Resting at the center of the sun disk,
the gnosis body is to be held as blazing"

means that on the pericarp of the lotus, upon a sun, one sets the body of the wisdom ḍākinī or her seed syllable. In

"In the fire of the knot, in white,
Vajraḍākinī is to be dissolved,"

the fire of the knot is this: on the eastern petal of the lotus that rests in the middle of the fire blazing from the hearth of the knot is Vajraḍākinī, white in color, or her seed syllable. To the right is the Fierce Lady, yellow, or her seed syllable. In the west is Vetālī, red, or her seed syllable. To the left is Caṇḍikā, or her seed syllable.

"The fire is the nature of the fifth"

refers to Vajraḍākinī. Some texts read "five," and there all five are to be included. The rest is easy to understand.

In the passage beginning "water and," water is in the east, fire in the south, wind in the west and earth in the north; space and pure consciousness are in the center. The passage beginning

"In the nature of the five goddesses"

means that, having meditated on the fire goddesses at the knot at the navel, one should perform the fire offering with the stream of nectar from hūṃ.

"In the center the nectar is to be visualized"

means that, having meditated on the nectar drawn up from the eighteen sites, one should let the nectar fall into the fire moment by moment. This is what is taught in the passage beginning "the cow's hoof." "The cow's hoof" is the center of the eyebrows. "Dripping from its center" is the stream of nectar of the hūṃ that rests there. The passage beginning

"In the middle of the fire, the basis for burning"

shows the letter aṃ, whose nature is fire. The meaning is that its light, stirred by the moving wind, touches the drop of hūṃ, and through the dripping that results, one performs the fire offering of nectar.

"For one's own increase one should burn the offering"

means that by meditating in this way it becomes rasāyana, an elixir of rejuvenation. If one asks from what, the text says:

"A little food, drink and the like,"

and "having made it suitable" means having purified it. The passage beginning "Practicing in this way" means that for whatever yogin enjoys food in that sequence, it does not become a debt; that is, it does not become a debt to the patron. In the passage beginning "the Secret Seat," "section" is so called because this text, beginning from the meditation on the nectar and going up to the secret fire offering, sets it out and makes it clear.

This concludes the second section of the Secret Seat, from the commentary on the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha called Smṛtinibandha.

Now, in order to become accustomed to the winds and to know the measures and so on of the distinctions of the elements, the passage beginning "the monkey and" is stated. "Not staying still, like a monkey" refers to wind and mind, because they are by nature always moving. "Sameness" means remaining steady. "By which" means by which samādhi.

"The characteristics of the joining of the body"

are the characteristics of joining the wind of the body and the mind evenly into one. Alternatively, in the fifth section the request was made:

"Of water, fire and wind,
what is the measure of the body?"

In order to remind the Blessed One of that very question, the request beginning "the joining of the body" is made. The answer is given in the passage beginning

"The distinctions of the elements,
and the secret, hear from the great vajra."

"The distinctions of the elements" is the wind moving outward. "The secret" is the wind entering inward. The meaning is: hear of these as nondual with the mind.

"The mind abides in sameness"

means that it abides by being nondual with the mind. If one asks how, the text says:

"Having fully subsided, it enters kāli."

"Fully subsided" is the wind going outward. kāli is wisdom. "Enters" means abides as its nature; that is, the outgoing wind has the nature of wisdom. In the same way, the wind entering inward is to be understood as having the nature of skillful means.

With these lines the text shows the following. The mind itself rides upon the wind. Because it goes out and comes in, the wind itself is consciousness, and the two are indivisible. Therefore, sent out in the form of wind, it takes up the nectar of the ten directions, comes back again, enters, and abides at the heart as the mind itself. Meditating in this way on the going out and coming in again and again, one should gladden the mind of the mind; and through the union of wisdom and skillful means it is to be held at the secret place. As it is also said:

"Always, through wisdom and skillful means,
it is accomplished with joy and bliss."

Therefore, as is stated in the guru's oral instruction, when one reaches the culmination of this familiarization, one comes to possess the ten powers and the rest.

Now, to teach the measures of water and the other elements, "from the body" is stated. "The hairs growing" means that the hairs of the head and the other hairs grow; "the measure of water" means the sign of water. "Everywhere" is the sign establishing that water pervades the whole body, because the sprouting of the head hair and the other hairs is the result of oiliness and moisture.

"Water is not the body"

means that it is not a proof establishing water as the body. If one asks what then, the passage beginning

"In the body is the measure of water"

is stated, establishing that water is present, pervading the entire body. The rest is easy to understand. To show that this water also has the nature of Vajrā, the passage beginning "endowed with intelligence" is stated. "Measure" is the nature of water; also, it is a measure because it is established by a means of valid cognition.

In the passage beginning "the limbs of the body," "the ripening of food" is the digestion of food; that is the sign that fire is present in the body, by its nature of warmth. In the passage beginning "the openings," the openings are the nostrils and the rest. "Like vapor" is the movement of the breath; that is the proof that wind pervades the whole body.

In the passage beginning "Earth," "it is the abode of all" means that it is the support and abode of all things, water and the rest. Otherwise it would follow, absurdly, that water and the rest would not be perceived in the body. It is with this in mind that in the fifth section only the three elements were explicitly asked about.

"Endowed with the qualities of form and so on and the faculties"

means that objects and faculties, the qualities of earth, rest upon earth. Because it is adorned and ornamented by those qualities, the text continues up to "the chief." To sum up the proof, the text says:

"Because form and so on appear."

Since the supported things, form and the rest, water and the rest, and the faculties, appear and are perceived in the body, one should know that the nature of their support, earth, is present. For that very reason the text says, "Earth is the basis of proof," showing it as the support of the reasons.

"Because it is endowed with qualities,
it is widely known as the Beautiful Lady"

is an explanation of the name.

In the passage beginning "The mind," "cutting off doubt" is said because the mind makes the characteristics of earth and the rest known.

"It is the agent, the gatherer and also the enjoyer"

means that it is the agent of the actions of body and the rest, virtuous, nonvirtuous and mixed, because it performs them. It is also the gatherer, because it turns back from them. It is also the enjoyer, because it experiences their results. Through these proofs one infers that the mind is present in the body. Otherwise it would follow, absurdly, that engaging in virtuous and sinful actions and so forth would not appear in the body.

To teach the second reason, the passage beginning "the faculties" is stated. "The minds of all the faculties" are the consciousnesses. "The bases of characterization" are the objects. "The characteristics are to be known" means that consciousness comprehends the objects; this is that reason. To make this clear and to establish the expression "king of the faculties," the passage beginning

"The king of the faculties enjoys what is to be used"

is stated. If one asks what it enjoys, it is pleasures, that is, pleasure and the like. If one asks why, it is through the use of objects and the rest, that is, because it experiences the pleasures and the like that come from objects and faculties being used. The intent is that the inference that what is meant by "gnosis is to be relied on in the body" is present is also called "the king of the faculties."

Now, to teach the proof that all of these are gathered into consciousness itself, the passage beginning "The mind" is stated.

"If water were something else"

means different from consciousness.

"Why, when one dies, does water not arise?"

teaches an invalidating proof. "When one dies" means in the dead body. "Water does not arise" means that it is not perceived. "Why" is a word of reasoning; the intent is that it would absurdly follow that it would still be perceived.

"In the body that is parted from gnosis"

means in the dead body.

"The breath of the mind does not move"

means that the movement of the wind that sets consciousness in motion is not perceived.

"It does not sink within water"

is the reason establishing that in the dead body there is no heaviness, the characteristic of earth. To teach the summary, the passage beginning

"Because there are five measures in the body"

is stated. The meaning is that because the five measures appear in the body that has mind, they are gathered into consciousness as their root.

"Having understood the characteristic as one,
the yogin should examine beings"

and so on means that, having understood all phenomena as having the nature of consciousness, one should rely on a spiritual friend in order to understand its nature.

"The gnosis of objects and faculties
becomes bound through torment"

is the examination of beings, the consciousness that is bound in saṃsāra. The meaning is that the engagement of the faculties and consciousness with objects, bound together with torment, binds one in saṃsāra. "Torment" is afflicting body and mind with ascetic observances, austerities and the like.

Then, if one asks by what restraint one becomes victorious over saṃsāra, the passage beginning "egg-born" is stated. "Abiding in the center" is consciousness, because it abides at the heart. "Egg-born" means that its abode is an egg: it is bound in the abode of the heart blood, which resembles an egg.

The practice of restraint is taught. In

"With the mind, going out and not going out,
the moving thing is to be turned,"

"going out and not going out" are the breath going out and coming in. "With the mind" means accompanied by mind. "The moving thing" means that wind and consciousness are unstable; this is to be joined with "is to be bound." If one asks by what, it is by turning, that is, by familiarization with the wind going out and not going out. In the passage beginning "bound," "the lotus seat" is a comfortable seat, that is, upon the lotus of the heart and the lotus of the source of phenomena. "For that reason" means the cause of this, the samādhi that is preceded by emptiness and the rest. "The nature of consciousness is to be indicated" means that everything is to be understood as mere appearance of the mind. If one asks how this becomes the yoga of the deity, "the reality called nature" is stated; the meaning is that through the joining of skillful means and wisdom it becomes the actual yoga of the deity.

Now the rite for becoming familiar with the going out and coming in of the wind is taught, in the passage beginning "at the navel." In

"At the navel, a plantain flower,"

the words "is to be meditated on" are to be supplied. The text then shows that one meditates on the letter a upon a lotus like a plantain flower at the navel.

"At the heart too it stands raised"

means that above it, at the heart, there is also something like an eight-petaled lotus facing downward.

"It is always the door of emptiness"

means that the lotus is slightly open.

"The wise should always turn it back"

shows that it faces downward. The rest is easy to understand.

"The joining of gnosis and consciousness"

means writing hūṃ in the place of that upper lotus. In

"In the eastern part of the joining,
six fingers in harmony with it,"

"the joining" is the navel lotus. "The eastern" is its upper part. To "six fingers" the words "the rays of fire" are to be supplied: the rays of fire blaze upward six fingers high. "In harmony with it" means helpful to the lotus of the heart.

"In the western part of the knot"

means below the knot at the navel. "Five fingers" is as before.

"In that field, from the seed
and the mass of light rays, the sprout blazes"

means that just as seeds and sprouts grow in the soil, so from the ground of the knot at the navel the sprout of fire from the seed, the letter aṃ, blazes six fingers and so on.

"With the light rays of that seed
it is to be encircled like a plantain"

means that one meditates on the seed of the upper lotus, the letter hūṃ, together with its light rays as well.

Some say that "At the navel stands a plantain flower" refers to the lotus of the heart. That plantain flower, as before, is slightly open and faces downward; since it stands above the navel, close to the navel, it is said to "stand at the navel." It is endowed with filaments; it has eight petals, and the light rays of the letter aṃ resting at its center make the heart lotus slightly open. They say that the rest is as explained before.

To show that the subtle light rays of the letter aṃ stimulate the letter hūṃ resting in the lotus of the heart, the text says:

"The seed of the five increases
is to be pressed from below the belly."

The seed of the five increases is the letter hūṃ. "Below the belly" means what rests at the navel. "Pressed" means that the lotus opens slightly and the hūṃ is stimulated.

"Beautifully adorned with eight petals"

refers to the eight petals of both lotuses. On the petals of the lower lotus, the eastern and the rest, one writes in order, turning to the left, the four suṃ kṣuṃ yuṃ hūṃ. In the center is aṃ. In the intermediate directions, beginning with Īśāna, one writes the four ymraṃ hmraṃ smraṃ kṣmaṃ. On the part of the rim one writes ṛ ṝ ḷ ḹ. On the upper lotus one is to write exactly the same, except that in the center one writes hūṃ.

Then, to show that the hūṃ is stimulated by the light rays of aṃ and that its stream of nectar washes the entire body above and below, the passage beginning "the cow's hoof" is stated. "The cow's hoof" is the body. "Into the nine doors" means filling all nine doors.

"Blazing light streams out from within the hairs"

means this: the seed of gnosis and consciousness is hūṃ; a stream of nectar then pours out from the pores of the body hair, washing the entire body.

To teach the recitation, the passage beginning "gnosis" is stated. The seed of gnosis and consciousness is hūṃ. When the wind goes out, the light rays of aṃ stimulate the hūṃ; and when the wind comes in, the stream from the hūṃ fills the entire body. At those times of the breath going out and coming in, one should recite the two, aṃ and hūṃ, which have the nature of wisdom and skillful means. To teach that one should become familiar with this continuously, in all circumstances, the passage beginning "going and" is stated; the meaning is that one does not abandon it even when partaking of food. The rest is easy to understand.

Now, to teach transference upward, the passage beginning "transference upward" is stated. "The drop" is at the center of the eyebrows. "Water" is the vajra door. "Not water" is the door of excrement.

"The navel is the desire heaven"

means that if consciousness departs by the path of the navel, one then becomes a god of the desire realm. "Having a body in the form of a drop" means being born as a god of the form realm. "From above" means from the golden door. "The upper abode" means in a higher abode; here too, just as with the hells, one should understand that birth comes swiftly. "The door of birth in existence" is the mouth. The meaning of the passage beginning

"From the distinctions of the doors"

is this: since by the distinctions of the doors the destinies are distinguished as excellent and not excellent, the yogin should become familiar with the samādhi of transference upward, and when he is able and the time has come, he should send consciousness out by the good path.

"Before one is struck by fever and the like"

shows that it is to be done six months before one's time runs out, because when one is struck by fever and the like one is unable to carry out the practice of transference upward, and because it is despised by worldly people.

"Having first undertaken the vase"

means that one should first become familiar with vase breathing. "For increase" refers to the letter hūṃ, yellow. "The door of the stūpa" is at the center of the eyebrows. "The lower door" is below it. "The seed of water" is the letter suṃ. "The doors of water and not water" have already been explained. "The seed of fire" is kṣuṃ. By the words "all the doors," furthermore, at the golden door there is kṣuṃ; in the two eyes, a blue hūṃ; in the ears and nostrils, a red yuṃ; in the mouth, a white suṃ.

"The body in the color of wind"

means that it is to be made blue.

"The mind upon the wind maṇḍala"

means meditating on hūṃ upon a wind maṇḍala at the heart. The meaning of the passage beginning

"At the root, the seed of wind"

is that one writes two letters yuṃ, above and below the wind maṇḍala. "The seed of vajra" is suṃ. "The seed of the Fierce Lady" is kṣuṃ. "The hook and so on" is the seed hi ka. In "it is to be joined," the seed hi is written below the seed yuṃ, and kā below the upper yuṃ. "Twenty-one breaths" means applying it twenty-one times after one has become familiar with it; by this, transference takes place. In

"From its place, it is to be drawn up by the Fierce Lady,"

"the Fierce Lady" is the letter aṃ, and "drawn up from its place" means that its light rays push the wind and mind of the heart upward. "From place to place" means pushing them upward through the heart, the throat and so on. Alternatively, it is "abiding in place," that is, abiding in all the limbs.

"For the ripening of the upper seed"

refers to binding white hair; this will be explained below.

"Purify with one and a half letters"

means reciting the seed hi ka. Some say the seeds are a and hūṃ.

"Uttering the roar of the Fierce Lady"

means that from below the light rays of the seed aṃ, stirred by the wind, strike the hūṃ at the heart. "The end of the eighth" is ha. "The first of the first group" is ka. "The fierce roar" is i. On the second interpretation, the fierce roar has already been explained; it is itself the first of the first group, and "the end of the eighth" is hūṃ. The rest has already been explained.
