In this practice, one first performs the deity yoga that comes below, preceded by meditation on emptiness and the rest. One seals the nine doors with hūṃ and the others and arranges the letters in order as described before. When the wind moves upward, the light of the seed aṃ resting at the navel, stirred by the wind, strikes the hūṃ at the heart, and pushes it together with the wind from the throat up to the head. When the wind enters downward, one imagines that it comes back again. In particular, when the wind enters downward, the places of the seed hi ka are to be reversed from before. For the recitation as well, one recites the seed hi ka itself. If one becomes familiar with it twenty-one times in this way, transference will certainly take place, and consciousness will transfer by whichever door one wishes.

To explain the rite for binding white hair, the passage beginning

"From above, likewise, in another place above"

is stated. "Above" is the thirty-two-petaled lotus within the head. "Another place above" means writing there a blue letter haṃ facing downward. "Likewise" means that with the light rays of the letter aṃ meditated on at the navel one stimulates the haṃ and makes a stream of nectar fall; by this, white hair will certainly be bound.

"The upper root is to be eaten"

teaches an external practice: if one eats dried dung together with menstrual blood and semen, this will bind white hair. The rest is easy to understand.

"Just as the lotus born from the mud
is stainless and delightful"

means that just as the lotus is not soiled by water, mud, slime and the like, so too is the gnosis body. The rest is easy to understand. The passage beginning

"Through the distinctive accomplishment of yoga"

teaches deity yoga; its meaning, too, is simply to be understood.

To teach another form of transference, the passage beginning "Then, the external" is stated. "External" means other, that is, other than what was taught before. "The two places" are the two places of the navel and the heart; "is to be contemplated" means contemplating as set out below. That very thing is taught in the passage beginning "the wind maṇḍala." "The vowels and consonants" is the letter hūṃ at the heart.

"Its seed is to be set down as red"

refers to the red seed aṃ at the navel. "Going out, not going out" means that, as the breath goes out, the seed of consciousness is placed within the head.

"Recite by entering and not entering"

means turning it back again as the breath comes in. "Recite" means to familiarize oneself. Alternatively, as the breath goes out and comes in, one recites the seed hi ka, reversed from that, at the junction of the throat.

With these lines the text teaches the following. One imagines at the navel, upon a wind maṇḍala, a fire maṇḍala, and upon that a red aṃ like the color of the sun. One meditates on a blue hūṃ facing downward on the lotus of the heart. When the wind goes out, then, like a plantain flower stirred by the wind, the letter hūṃ together with its lotus turns to face upward and is pushed into the head; when the wind comes in, it is drawn back again. By becoming familiar with this again and again, transference takes place without difficulty.

Now, to teach the practice of the inner rite, the passage beginning "Then" is stated. "External" is as explained before.

"The practitioner emanates"

means emanating for the sake of the practitioner. If one asks what, the text says:

"In accord with the mind of the other."

The meaning of these lines is this. One imagines at one's own navel a red letter aṃ resting on fire and wind, and meditates on a red hūṃ at the heart of the one to be subjugated. The light rays of the aṃ go out from one's own right nostril and enter the left nostril of the one to be subjugated, seize the consciousness of the heart, come out of that person's right nostril, and enter one's own left nostril; and one meditates on it being burned within the fire. By reciting in this way again and again, one comes to know the mind of the other, and that person will remain mute until death. This is the meaning. The rest is easy to understand.

On the passage beginning "the Secret Seat": because this text clearly teaches habituation to the winds, the inference that water and the other elements abide in the body, and so forth, it is a chapter. From the commentary on the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha called Smṛtinibandha, this is the third section, that of the Secret Seat.

Now, in order to teach what is more secret even than the secret, the passage beginning "of the Secret Seat" is stated; it is mostly easy to understand. In order to teach another way of seizing consciousness, the passage beginning "delightful" is stated, and by the passage beginning "the moon maṇḍala" the yoga of the wisdom ḍākinī is taught.

On "In the center meditate on the chief of melting": it is "melting" because it dissolves; it is the letter hūṃ. "The seed of Akṣobhya" is aṃ. "The jewel seed" is āṃ. The seed of Amitābha is eṃ. The seed of Amoghasiddhi is khaṃ. In order to teach this, the passage beginning "the first vowel" is stated. Alternatively, in "four" the suffix is unexpressed, and here it should be construed as "the fourth"; this teaches that the letter aṃ is to be written in the center of the navel lotus. In that case the seeds of Akṣobhya and the others are hūṃ, āṃ, jṝṃ and khaṃ. The passage beginning "the intermediate directions" applies to both. The rest is easy to understand.

On the passage beginning "of emptiness": if it is asked, "If everything is empty, what would become the essence?", the passage beginning "the plantain" is spoken. Just as from the plantain, which has no core, an essence of nectar arises, so from meditating on the seeds and so forth, which have no core, the fruit of the wisdom body of emptiness ripens.

On the passage beginning "by which": "Having come to know whose characteristics" means having understood, through hearing and reflection, the intrinsic nature of all aspects. "Swiftly" means in this very life. In order to teach another form of the meditative absorption of summoning nectar, the passage beginning "of the five expanded" is stated. "The seed of nectar" is soṃ. This teaches meditating on the letter hūṃ on a moon maṇḍala above the letter soṃ. "In the center of the drop" means in the center of the brows; there too the seed is hūṃ. "Make it round, the size of a thumb" refers to the letter tha, which has the form of a moon maṇḍala; in its center one writes the hūṃ of the ashes, as above. "The seed in its center" means in the center of the navel; there one writes the seed of the vowels and consonants, the letter aṃ. The passage beginning "the tip of the hair" teaches the gradual impelling by the light rays of aṃ.

In order to teach this, the passage beginning "the drop" is stated. "Look to the station of the drop's place" teaches the drop of the navel lotus. "In the heart, at the door of Brahmā, and at the doors of the path of the drop" is connected with "wisdom and consciousness are to be seized": one writes the letter hūṃ at the heart, between the brows, and at the golden door.

Others say: the place of the drop is the navel; the station is the throat; the door of Brahmā is itself the path of the drop, that is, the crown of the head. "Wisdom and consciousness are to be seized" means tasting and beholding the nectar in those places. How so?

"In the center of the moon, the seed:
make it round, the size of a thumb.
The seed in its center
is drawn from the vowels and consonants."

Connecting this with what came before, these are to be understood as aṃ, hūṃ, eṃ and haṃ. So they say.

Here mantra recitation is taught: the passage beginning "for the enjoyments of existence, form and the rest" is spoken. It means seeking the enjoyments of the desire realm, the form realm and the formless realm; or else, it is for the purification of form, sound and the rest. "Entering all around" refers to the drop and the crescent moon. "Head and not-head" are ha and u. "Always fasten it at the root" means joining u to the root of ha; combined, it becomes hūṃ. For that very reason the passage beginning "wisdom" is stated; it is easy to understand. Others say that "head and not-head" are a and ha, because they are the first and last of all the letters. "Entering all around" is the crescent and the drop. Combined, they become aṃ haṃ. For that very reason the passage beginning "wisdom" is stated: aṃ haṃ are the seeds.

The five kinds of signs are taught: the passage beginning "like a lamp" is stated, and it is easy to understand. In order to teach the suchness of the stūpa, the passage beginning "stūpa" is stated. The meaning of "at the heart" is this: having meditated on the letter hūṃ on a moon at the heart, one burns all things with its light rays, and from the light rays of the ashes one generates, by means of the letter hūṃ, the form of a stūpa.

The purity of the deities is taught. On the passage beginning "zigzag": "zigzag" means the stepped tiers. "Earth" means the earth maṇḍala. "It is the element of the eight directions" means firmly resolving that it has the nature of the mundane and supramundane deities of the eight directions who abide on the earth maṇḍala. "And again" means the eight bodhisattvas, Kṣitigarbha and the rest. "The eighteen embodied ones" means that, having shown the abode of Maheśvara (Śiva), one makes it eighteenfold. Some say that hūṃ itself is the form of the stūpa. The passage beginning "giving and moral discipline" has been explained before. "Nectar" is the nectar of equality.

On "It is the yoga of the dharmadhātu": dharma means the aggregates and so forth; dhātu means the support, that is, the body; its yoga is the yoga of emptiness. Alternatively, the dharmadhātu is emptiness, and the yoga is its body, the stūpa.

Again, another application is taught. On the passage beginning "great secret": the passage beginning "the five expanded" has already been explained in the section on where transference takes place. "In the door at its center, white like a conch and the moon" is the letter hūṃ. "Of the four elements in the four directions" refers, as mentioned before, to hūṃ, suṃ and the rest. "Four stacked seeds in the intermediate directions" refers to hmraṃ and the rest. "Afterwards, form is to be destroyed" teaches equality.

In order to explain this in detail, the passage beginning "together with the egg-born, it is to be destroyed" is stated; it is easy to understand. "The seeds of the vowels and consonants" are aṃ and hūṃ. "The circle of the land" means the body. "The fruit of stream entry" is the fruit of drinking the nectar; it is to be connected with "liberating from saṃsāra."

The excellent place is taught: the passage beginning "the practitioner, free of bustle." If one asks what one is to live on there, the passage beginning "na ga mū la" is stated. na ga means the strength of an elephant; it is the plant known by the name "cowherd's fruit," and its root is to be taken. Of the palāśa, the flower is to be taken. Costus is simply as understood. "Three parts" means taking three srang of each one separately. "And magadha" means long pepper, of which one srang is taken. "In equal parts" means mixing their powders together. "A quarter" means a quarter of a srang, that is, eating two zho at a time. "Guarding the discipline" means that by relying on the medicine one turns away from outer foods and the like. Alternatively, it teaches that by relying on the medicine one restrains and guards the senses and so possesses the activity of retreat.

In order to produce confidence in the mantras, mudrās, deities and so forth, and in order to teach that meditation, mantra recitation and the rest are to be practiced until they become manifest, the passage beginning "just as" is stated. The meaning of the first half of the verse is this: young girls wait for the talk of young boys, and having heard their talk, they rejoice in it with an experience of joy, and they tell stories of sleeping with them and so forth, yet they do not actually take them as lovers. The passage beginning "thus the path is explained" shows that if one explains a path similar to that, there will be no bondage. "Yoga" means the rite. For that very reason it says, "It is not taught as an evil path."

Alternatively, if the path of this tantra is taught to women, children and the like, who delight in tales such as fables, there will be complete bondage; a is to be construed as "completely." Therefore for them it becomes an evil path. The passage beginning "the Veda" is easy to understand. "The gods of evil places" are the gods of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas). If one asks why one should not do those things, the passage beginning "empowerment" is stated; it is easy to understand.

On the passage beginning "te na hūṃ": te refers to the entities of the aggregates, elements and sense sources, which have the nature of body, speech and mind. na is a negation: there is no entity such as the grasped object. If one asks, "Then what is there?", hūṃ is stated, up to "it is mere appearance of the mind." In order to purify the body and so forth, the mantra is to be recited three times. The rest has already been explained.

In order to teach the great nature of the merit of the tantra, the passage beginning "a letter, or" is stated. On "the Secret Seat": "secret" means what is taught here, because it is to be kept utterly hidden; "seat" means its basis. The rest is easy to understand.

Whatever merit has arisen from what I have done
with great effort on this commentary on the Four Seats,
by it, like the stainless moonlight of an autumn night,
may beings realize the supreme meaning and abide in it.

The commentary on the king of tantras, the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha, called Smṛtinibandha, composed by the great master Bhavabhadra, is complete. It was established and translated by the Indian paṇḍita Gayadhara and the Tibetan chief editor-translator Gö Lhetsé.

Translator's notes

The Sanskrit "eighteen hundred thousand" and "twelve thousand" are given in the Tibetan without a unit; "verses" is supplied as the usual measure of a tantra's length. The image of "the lotus stalk and the lotus root" is literally "pad and the root of padma"; the sense seems to be that the tantra, like the lotus, is hollow or sparse in places and full in others. In the verse about the deer, the phrase rendered "cropping grass" could also be read as "cutting at the root." The phrase "the purpose is to become manifest as the deity of bliss" renders a terse and possibly abbreviated Tibetan phrase. The commentary uses the same phrase "its excellence of rgyud" twice; it is rendered "mindstream" for the Lord of wisdom and "tantra" for the net of yoginīs, as the contexts suggest. The eight retinue names in quotation marks are the root tantra's coded epithets, which the commentary identifies with the eight great bodhisattvas. The commentary glosses the root tantra word by word; quoted words and verse lines are the root text's. The remark about "putting a short vowel in place of a long one" concerns the Sanskrit spelling of a word in the root text and is not visible in Tibetan. The nāḍikā is a period of a water clock, here equated with the daṇḍa, sixty of which make a day and night. The twelve names of the divisions of the day (Rohita, Mohita, Bhadrā, Vṛṣabha, Kurma, Makara, Raṇḍa, Mikira, Vidrika, Vyākuli, Svapna, Kama) are given in the Tibetan as transliterated Sanskrit and are reproduced as spelled; several (Vidrika, Mikira, Kama) may be imperfect transmissions of the original names. The "units" are nāḍikās, which total sixty for the day and night. The verse on the eighteen fields of knowledge lists the Indian sciences, including the six Vedic limbs (phonetics, here called "training," ritual, grammar, etymology, prosody and astronomy); its items are given as this text states them. The remark that "sixty or thirty" is to be read as sixty-three is obscure, and the rendering of the oral instruction's method is tentative. The "imitation pearls" renders an uncertain Tibetan term. The opening name is spelled Bhitrikaga in this piece; since the later list of characteristics gives Vidrika in the same position, it is taken as that name, the final syllable ga probably being a slip for the particle la. "Weak in wisdom" takes shes rab zhen pa ("attached to wisdom") as a slip for shes rab zhan pa, which the gloss "foolish" requires. In the list of lunar mansions, the text as transmitted places Viśākhā and Svātī, and Śatabhiṣā and Dhaniṣṭhā, in an order that differs from the usual sequence; the names are given as the Tibetan writes them. The time unit "water hour" renders chu tshod (Skt. nāḍikā or ghaṭikā). The numbers in the verse on water hours are written in the Tibetan as "twelve" and "thirteen" (bcu gnyis, bcu gsum), but the commentary explains them as "ten taken twice" and "ten taken thrice," giving twenty-four and thirty-six; the translation follows the commentary's reading. The phrase "then one turns back" (de'i tshe slar ldog go) is terse; in context it probably means that the case is beyond cure and treatment is not undertaken. "Water hour" renders the Tibetan for the ghaṭikā or nāḍī, one sixtieth of a day and night, kept consistent with the preceding piece. Viṣu is the equinox. The figures given for each month (for example thirty-four day hours for both the first and the last month of autumn) are translated exactly as the Tibetan states them. "Both" refers to the two sets of eighteen from the previous piece: eighteen multiplied by ten gives a hundred and eighty each; the two hundreds give 200, the two eighties give 160, written as "16", and with two zeros appended the figure 21,600 results. The Tibetan rendered "the reckoning of the water day" is unusual and its exact sense is uncertain. The gloss on "Just as by the first a, so the last should be taken" names the letter a twice as it stands; one would expect the second to be the last vowel, but it is translated as written. "Calculations and divinations" renders an unclear phrase that appears to be a scribal variant for calculation and divination. "Fragment of skull" renders an uncertain expression. The title Smṛtinibandha is given as in the Sanskrit title of this commentary. "The Fierce Ḍākinī" translates the Tibetan name literally; it likely renders a Sanskrit name such as Raudrī, but this cannot be confirmed here. The phrase rendered "with a terrifying stream of nectar" is unusual in the Tibetan, and its exact sense is uncertain. The seed syllables are given letter for letter as the Tibetan spells them, including those the edition places in brackets. "The Gentle Supreme One" renders a Tibetan epithet whose Sanskrit original is uncertain (possibly Mañjuvara); the commentary identifies it with Ratnasambhava. In "all, homage, the burner at the end," the root word for "burner" is spelled as a near homograph meaning "craving," but the commentary reads it as "burner" (svāhā), which is followed here. "Devadatta" is the conventional placeholder for the name of the person protected. The goddesses of the four neuter vowels, the Blazing Lady, the Sucking Lady and the Ravisher, are rendered literally from the Tibetan, as their Sanskrit names cannot be identified with confidence. The seed syllables are given letter for letter as the edition spells them, including those it places in brackets; the Caṇḍālī mantra is given as written, without a closing svāhā. The mantra in the bowls contains the stock name Devadatta, which stands for the name of the person to be protected; the same placeholder name appears in the sample question of the messenger. The phrase rendered "the four horned ones" (rwa can bzhi) is obscure; the following passage explains the four applications from the guru's instructions as mikiri and the like. "Seal" renders rgya ldan, which may here mean a lock or sealing device on the door. Mikiri or Mikira, Bitika, Svapna and Kama appear to be technical names of inauspicious periods or conditions from the guru's instructions; they are given as the Tibetan spells them. The Hundred-Headed One is a literal rendering of a nāga name I cannot identify with confidence. The eighth nāga, rendered literally as "the Coiled Faller," would correspond in the standard list of eight nāga kings to Ananta, but the Tibetan name is not the usual one, so it is translated literally. In "with the seeds, remove the mind poison," an extra stray syllable before "mind" has been disregarded. Where the text reads "into the water" followed by an unclear verb, it is taken as "transferring," matching the verb used just after. "The sixth case in the sense of the second" means a genitive used for an accusative. The eight clouds are given with literal English names (the Thunderer, the Murky One, the Fierce One, the Coiling One, the Dense One, the Violent One, the Rain-Bringer, the Brimming One), since I cannot identify their Sanskrit forms with confidence. The gate nāgas are paired, two crossed at each of four gates; the east pair is read as Jaya and Bhadra. "The heart" is glossed in the Tibetan by a word that normally means a mark or sign and here most plausibly refers to the sexual organ, paired with the anus that follows; this is uncertain. The four applications are spelled differently here from their first mention; they are given in the forms used earlier, Mikira, Bitika, Svapna and Kama. "Purification, torma, touching" renders a terse list of ancillary rites whose third item is uncertain. The Self Seat is the Ātmapīṭha, the first of the four seats of the Catuṣpīṭha. The root verses gloss "going" (Tib. 'gro ba, likely Skt. gati) as "intrinsic nature," so it is rendered literally as "the going" rather than as "beings." "Sattva" in the root verse is explained by the commentator either as "the mind" or as a vocative addressed to Vajrapāṇi; both readings are kept. The verse lines on the father, mother, brahmins and king use deliberately transgressive coded language that the commentary decodes as delusion, craving, attachment and aversion, and pride. The quotation "Everything arises from pride itself" echoes the Sāṃkhya doctrine of ahaṃkāra, which fits the commentator's remark that the teaching is shared with non-Buddhists. The Tibetan reads "a single shoulder-load of emptiness" (stong pa nyid phrag gcig), but the commentary explains it as an illustration of countless former lives, so the root word is evidently stong phrag gcig, "one thousand"; it is translated accordingly. The gloss of "the beast" as "the owl" is translated as written. The bracketed seed syllables in the source are given letter for letter. "Urge on" in the root verse is explained by the commentary as if it read "upon the form" (the Tibetan words are near-homophones); the commentary's sense is followed. In the explanation of the Vajra Lady's seed, the Tibetan reads "vajra-fire" where "Vajra Lady" is evidently meant (a one-letter slip); translated as "the Vajra Lady." The letters assembled for the Vajradhara mantra spell out vajra kartari chinda chinda ("vajra-knife, cut, cut"), though the text itself does not state the completed form beyond the last words. "The letter of the strong dot" renders a literal Tibetan expression that the commentary identifies with the letter va; its exact technical sense is unclear. The assembled binding mantra is given letter for letter as the commentary spells it. "The Seat of the Other" (Parapīṭha) is the second of the tantra's four seats, following the Self Seat. The passage on the height of the Kailāsa mound reads literally "a half cubit endowed with a half," which the commentator says comes to two and a half spans; the arithmetic is translated as written. The goddesses' "emblems" here are hand gestures drawn or depicted on the petals. The sentence explaining "their measure" appears to run together the gloss on "their measure" (the emblems, measured and set up in each hearth) with the gloss on "with distinction" (the distinction of the rites of pacifying and so on); it is rendered as it stands. The identification of the seed of Siṃhinī as "the leader" (ded dpon) and of "livestock" as the owl follows the Tibetan literally. "The seat of union" is glossed as the cross-legged posture of the sattva (sattvaparyaṅka). The wisdom and pledge beings here are given in the feminine (sems ma), matching the ḍākinī as chosen deity. "Withdrawn into one's own center" renders rang gi dbus as written; one would expect "into one's own body" (lus), but the text is kept as it stands. "The great black one" in the fierce-rite list is taken, as the commentary directs, as the name of a fruit, not the deity Mahākāla. Lungtang and the "pine" (thang shing) are plant names left in simple form. "Especial" (khyad par) is glossed in two alternative ways by the commentator, both kept. Lungtang, "the crooked one" and "the golden tree" are kept as given in the preceding passage. "The circle of goddesses" renders lha mo 'khor lo, taken as the goddesses' maṇḍala within the vase. "Every morning" takes nang re zhing in the sense of nang as "morning." "Rājagṛha" is kept, as the house glossary directs, though here the context ("ministers and so on") suggests the phrase is meant generally as "the royal palace." The root verse cited as "form is ... empty" reads stod in the Tibetan, but the commentary glosses it as stong, "empty," which is translated. Likewise the root line "speech too abides in equality" reads dag ("pure") where the commentary's explanation (body, mind and speech) requires ngag, "speech." In the root line "some view them as equal" the root has 'ga' zhig "some," but the commentary glosses 'ba' zhig "solely," suggesting a variant reading of the root. In the explanation of body and mind, mi rang ba is read as mi rung ba, "not possible." The verse "Existence and nonexistence..." is from the Samādhirāja Sūtra; "Not from self, not from other..." is Nāgārjuna's Mūlamadhyamakakārikā 1.1. In the explanation of the ten places, the Tibetan reads "likewise ... likewise" in a doubled, garbled phrase (de bzhin su de bzhin du), evidently a copyist's repetition; it is translated once. The Tibetan mtshan khung in the mudrā verse is taken in its usual sense, "armpit." "Commitment goddesses" and "wisdom goddesses" render the feminine forms of samayasattva and jñānasattva. The root line rendered "In the east, Ḍākinī, O Lord of Victors" reads rgyal dbang mo; the commentary takes rgyal dbang as an address to Vajrapāṇi, which is followed here. "Faces in keeping with their own names" reads gdang, taken as gdong, "face." The opening explains a coded mantra-extraction: "wind" and "fire" stand for the letters ya and ra, and "the seventh" from each is counted along the semivowel-sibilant series. The sentence identifying the seed of the Vajra Lady is terse and its exact reference is uncertain; it is translated as it stands. "The hook of the various" is glossed by a fivefold code, go ku da ha na, which in this literature abbreviates the five meats (cow, dog, elephant, horse and human). "Twenty offerings" refers to the series of twenty offering gestures that the text goes on to describe. In the gloss on the line "Bow to the yoginīs' rapture," the commentary's lemma reads ཐུད, which makes no sense here; it is evidently a slip for འདུད ("bow"), the word of the root line, and is translated so. Likewise ཕྱག་འཚོལ is read as ཕྱག་འཚལ ("pay homage"). ཀྱེའི་རྡོ་རྗེ in the gloss on "Vajra" is treated as the vocative he vajra ("O Vajra"), which is also the form of the name Hevajra; both are given. The verb ཀུན་རྟོག in the root line is glossed by the commentary as realization (ཀུན་དུ་རྟོགས), hence "discerning" rather than "conceptualizing." Several of the worldly field protectors in the nine-square torma maṇḍala could not be identified with confidence and are rendered by meaning: "the Gnashing One," "the Hideous One," and "the One Beyond Austerities"; likewise "the Crone," one of the thirty-two yoginīs. Black Rudra, Great Rudra and Black Devadatta render Kṛṣṇarudra, Mahārudra and Kṛṣṇadevadatta. The text lists seven names for the surrounding squares of a nine-square grid; this is given as it stands. "The second case is used in the sense of the third" is a grammatical remark: an accusative-type form in the root line functions as the instrumental (agent). The imperative ཐོང in "In this there is no conceptuality: let it go!" is ambiguous (it can be read as "release," "see," or "drink"); it is rendered "let it go," which fits the commentary's gloss "one taste" of conceptions. The commentary on "Mount Meru" speaks of "seven Merus," rendered as it stands. The lemma "by the power of churning" is read from the commentary's own gloss (བསྲུབས), the root citation being spelled བསྒྲུབས. The root line reads "enjoy it truly" (བདེན་པར), but the commentary's gloss reads "with pleasure" (བདེ་བར); the commentary's reading is followed. In the pointing gesture, "praising" (བསྟོད) makes no sense before "binding a fist" and is evidently a slip for a word meaning "clenching"; it is rendered accordingly. The southwestern guardian is here spelled དགའ་ལས་འདས་པ ("Beyond Joy"), apparently the same figure earlier spelled དཀའ་ལས་འདས་པ, and the same rendering "the One Beyond Austerities" is kept. Many of the minor yoginī names (the Good, Great and Stout Black Ladies, the Moon Lady, the Hateful Lady, the Hanging Lady, the Lamp Lady, the Powerful Lady) could not be identified with confidence and are translated by meaning. Jayā, Vijayā, Ajitā and Aparājitā and Umā are standard identifications. The "hooks" are code names for the five meats of the secret offering, identified by the initial or final letter of their Sanskrit names (na, ga, śva at the beginning, ha, śva at the end). The guardian here written "Beyond Those" (དེ་དག་ལས་འདས་པ) is evidently the One Beyond Austerities (དཀའ་ལས་འདས་པ) of the earlier list, the eighth of the nine squares being the vināyaka; it is translated so. In the gloss on "dust," the text reads "those without afflictions" (ཉོན་མོངས་པ་མེད་པ), which contradicts the sense; it is read as "the afflictions." The many retinue goddesses are translated by meaning, as they could not be identified with confidence. The phrase rendered "special elaborate preparations, the principal ones and so on" (mngon par 'du ba'i khyad par gtso bo la sogs pa) is obscure; it may instead refer to particular conditioned qualities with the principal one first. The verse line rendered "Do everything in a concealed and concise way" reads literally "gather everything into the sūtra/summary and act." The section on the seed syllables is a word puzzle built on Sanskrit: "the goose" (haṃsa) yields the syllable haṃ together with sa; "kāli" is read as kalā, a digit of the moon; and "the consumer" points to kṣa through kṣaya, "destruction." The commentary also plays on dbang po, "lord," and dpang po, "witness." The identification of "shape" with the letter sa follows the Tibetan as written and is obscure. The commentary works through Sanskrit wordplay: āli or ali and bhramara both mean "bee," and the syllables are etymologized separately (li as "taking," bhrama as "confusion" or "wandering," ra as "destroying" or "sporting"). "Hevajra" renders dgyes pa'i rdo rje. The phrase "because one abides for a long time" is terse in the Tibetan. The commentary relies heavily on Sanskrit etymologies: paryanta, "reaching to the limit," is split into pari, "all around," and anta; dharma is derived from "holding"; kāli is split into ka, "bliss," and li, "tasting." The "seven-horsed" is the sun (saptāśva), here decoded through wordplay into the syllables sa and ha. "The second case in the sense of the first" and "the seventh case in the sense of the fifth" refer to grammatical case usage in the root text. "Nasal discharge" renders dar snabs, whose exact sense here is uncertain. The sentence on the letter a composed of ta and ra rather than ta and ba concerns the shapes of letters in an Indic script and is obscure. "Sea monsters and fish ... kāli should be seen as being indicated" follows an awkward Tibetan sentence. The "five hooks" (pañcāṅkuśa) are the five meats, abbreviated "go ku da ha na": cow (go), dog (kukkura), elephant (dantin), horse (haya) and human (nara). The commentary's gloss on "speech" plays on Sanskrit go, which means both "cow" and "speech." The code names "Great Intellect," "rival of its kind" and "touch-goer" are translated literally. The Tibetan lud skabs, rendered "the excrement and the rest," is uncertain; it seems to sum up the five nectars encoded by the five buddha names. "With homage, and ending with craving" is a literal rendering of a coded root-text line, which the commentary glosses as adding oṃ and svāhā. "Half a watch" renders thun phyed, thun here being a division of the day (roughly three hours), not a meditation session. The commentary treats the Blazing Lady ('bar byed ma in the root lines, 'bar ma in the gloss) as one figure, so one English name is used for both. "Bring her into accord" renders mnyam par bya ba; it may be a slip for mnyes par bya ba, "to please," which the next sentence presupposes. The gloss on "the black one" ("bālataga and the names of the black tree," taken as a pair) is obscure; the plant name bālataga could not be identified with confidence and is given as written. "Other mustard seed" renders ske tshe, a second kind of mustard seed used in rites. Nīladaṇḍa is the Sanskrit name behind "the one with the blue staff"; the commentary explains "Blue" as an abbreviation of that name. "tritasa" is the Sanskrit word as the commentary quotes it, apparently standing for trayodaśa, "thirteen," which it glosses as "the deities." "The Inciting Lady" renders skul byed ma, whose Sanskrit name could not be identified with confidence. Ākāśagarbha is the commentary's own identification of the figure it calls Gaganagañja. The commentary's glosses on vimukta (vi as "conceptual thought," mukta as "respect," citing a list of verbal roots) are given as the text states them, though the last is philologically odd. "Fair-Colored," "Sweet-Voiced" and "Beautiful Lady" translate the root text's epithets, which the commentary itself identifies with Vasudhārā, the Inciting Lady and Bhṛkuṭī. The phrase rendered "with reference to the garment" (གོས་ལའོ) is obscure in this context and may be a slip; the sense is uncertain. Ābhāsvara here renders འོད་གསལ as the name of the second-dhyāna heaven, not the tantric "clear light." The list of form-realm heavens has sixteen named abodes plus a seventeenth class of subtle perception, as the text itself counts them. The opening words read "ni rnams kyi cho lo brgya," which makes no sense; the context (the reckoning of divine days against human years) plainly requires "mi rnams kyi lo brgya," "a hundred human years," and this is how it has been translated. In the gloss on "sameness, empty," the phrase "nyi tshe'i stong pa rnam par spyad pa" is obscure; it has been taken to mean that the emptiness intended goes beyond a partial emptiness to the emptiness of all things, possibly reading "spangs pa" (rejected) for "spyad pa." Devadatta (lha sbyin) is the conventional placeholder for the name of the person on whose behalf the rite is performed. The eight yoginīs are named in Tibetan translation; besides Ḍākinī and the Fierce Lady (kept as rendered earlier in this text), gtum mo, ro langs ma, seng ge mo, stag mo, ce spyang mo and 'ug pa mo have been given their usual Sanskrit equivalents Caṇḍikā, Vetālī, Siṃhinī, Vyāghrī, Jambukī (jackal) and Ulūkī (owl). The bracketed syllables in the edition are reproduced as written. The phrase "snang ba med pa'i stong pa las ltar bsgoms" is elliptical and has been rendered "meditating as in the case of emptiness without appearance." The alternation between kriṃ and kṛṃ follows the spelling of the Tibetan in each place. The name-slot in the mantra is the Tibetan "che ge mo," "so-and-so," and has been translated as such. The commentary identifies the third letter of the fifth class as pa, although in the Sanskrit order it would be ba; the text is translated as it stands. "Thig la" in the first gloss on bindu is read as "thig le," as in the parallel sentence that follows. The expression "gug skyed kun," glossed as oṃ, is obscure and has been rendered tentatively as "the all-producing curve." "Descent" renders dbab pa, the bringing down of wisdom into a subject (Skt. āveśa), the theme of the section that begins here. "Glo ru tshig gnyis" has been read as "glo ru tsheg gnyis," two dots at the side, that is, the visarga. "Descent" (Tibetan 'bebs pa, Skt. āveśa) refers to the rite of inducing possession, in which a "clear appearance" (gsal snang) descends upon the person being worked on. The text glosses "eight hundred" as one hundred and eight. The line rendered "One restores the self just as one wishes" is terse in the Tibetan; in context it concerns dismissing the possessing presence after the descent, but the exact sense is uncertain. In the explanation of the breath-arrow, the Tibetan reads "generates" (bskyed); in context "drives along" (bskyod) is the evident reading. In the final sentence, nas bya ba is read as zhes bya ba. Chegemo in the wind mantra is the Tibetan placeholder "so-and-so," where the target's name is inserted, and is translated outside the mantra. "Mantra garland" in the pacification rite likely means the written mantra string with the interspersed name. The text of the bhaga-binding root line says "lotus," which the commentary glosses as the triangular source of phenomena. "Seat of wind and of a preta" renders the Tibetan as it stands; it may mean a preta seated upon a wind maṇḍala. At the time of approach the Tibetan reads sngags brgya bar 'bum du; it is taken as reciting the mantra a hundred thousand times. Olma sé is a plant name given in phonetics, since its identification is uncertain. The "radiant" medicine is likewise rendered by its meaning. In the etymology of vajra, the Tibetan reads "yang na" ("alternatively") followed by "means many," where the Sanskrit syllable glossed as "many" seems to have dropped out; it is rendered as it stands. "Both Dharma and a bell" renders a text that repeats "dharma"; the repetition looks like a copyist's slip. "This is the second, by way of the sense of the third" is a literal rendering of an elliptical remark, apparently noting that this alternative reading counts as the second of the three kinds of patience. "In the section on ash" renders སྔར་ཐལ་བ་ལས་བསྟན་པ; the reference of "ash" (presumably an earlier passage of the commentary or root tantra) is uncertain. In "ས་བོན་ཨཱཿ་ས་མཚན" the ས after the seed syllable is read as an instrumental, "marked with". The commentator glosses "all sentient beings" as the wisdom consort and their "aim" as bliss. Karaṇa (ཀ་ར་ན) is kept as the technical term for the coital postures and movements described. The commentator reads "the secret place" (གསང་བ) as the neck on the authority of oral instruction, so it is translated that way where he applies it. "Extended to the word 'vajra'" renders བསྙེག, understood as carrying the word over to qualify Vajrasattva. "Karaṇa" and its shortened "kara" are kept as the technical term for the coital postures. The commentator glosses the unusual word ཐོང་ཀ as "shoulders"; it is left untranslated where he quotes it. In the second interpretation of "passionate attachment" he takes the root verse's Sanskrit citta na as "mind" plus a negation, so that the line describes the consort being without attachment. The stacked seed syllables are given letter for letter as printed; the source edition brackets them. The commentator explicitly assigns the syllable hūṃ to "earth" here, which is translated as it stands. "The nature of the twelve" is rendered literally; the reference is not explained in this piece. The refrain at the end of each line of the vajra song is given as the Tibetan spells it. ཆུ་ཤིང is taken as the plantain (kadalī), the classic image of something without a core. "Indra's hand" (dbang po lag pa) is a hand-shaped root, probably the salep orchid known in Tibetan medicine as dbang lag. The two plant names are given as the Tibetan spells them; the second is presumably śālaparṇikā. The root word glossed as "a seller of meat" is literally "deer" (sha ba); the commentary reads it through sha, "meat." In "pressing the heart with each of the two feet," whose heart is meant is not fully clear in the Tibetan. "Koko" renders the Tibetan ko sko, an unidentified gesture made by raising the index and middle fingers; its exact sense is uncertain. The explanation of "a" as a negation suggests the root verse reads a word like asamaya, "non-pledge." The opening clause is compressed; it may also be read as referring to those who were formerly deluded about the way of the Perfection Vehicle. "Great black ghee" and "great flesh" are the usual tantric code terms for human fat and human flesh. In the explanation of not stepping on the master's shadow, the text reads mchod ba ("worshipping"), which makes no sense here; it has been taken as mchong ba, "jumping over." Avīci is the lowest hell, of uninterrupted torment. "How it should be recited" renders ནན་ཏུར, which appears to be a scribal slip; the sense is clear from the answer that follows. In "arranging armor on the body," བཀོན is read as བཀོད, "arranged," as in the parallel phrase earlier in the passage. "The prediction" here is the master's prediction of the disciple's future awakening given at the close of empowerment. The repeated phrase rendered "in the other sense" or "as an exception" (གཞན་དུ་ན ... མ་གཏོགས) introduces the commentator's esoteric or exceptional reading of each precept, for example that the view of a self is what is to be "slain," and that the "taking what is not given" concerns the awakening mind in the consort's lotus. In "the view of a self ... is to be slain" the Tibetan has an instrumental, which could also be read "one should slay by means of the view of a self"; the reading given fits the pattern of the passage. "The blazing fire" here is the opening of a root-text line, not the goddess named the Blazing Lady elsewhere in the text. In "the ocean and the water in an ox's hoofprint," the Tibetan has only "the ocean and the ox's track"; the familiar simile of the vast ocean against the little water in a hoofprint is spelled out for clarity. "Measure" renders ཚད་མ (pramāṇa), which the commentary itself glosses here as "intrinsic nature," not in its epistemological sense of valid cognition. The phrase "There is no obtaining of the fruit" quotes a reading of the root verse that differs from the verse as cited just before ("even the yogin who obtains their fruit"); both are rendered as written. "Sky-goer" here renders མཁའ་འགྲོ in its literal sense, describing wind moving through the sky and, in the variant reading, karma and the afflictions that move without a body; it does not refer to the ḍākinīs. The text says that the eight hot hells "together with the eight cold hells are called the eight great hells," and that there are twenty abodes; this is translated as it stands. The quoted verse is from Vasubandhu's Thirty Verses (Triṃśikā), where the received Tibetan reads "latencies of karma" for this text's "latencies of appropriation." "Displaying all" renders ཐམས་ཅད་འཚོད, whose verb is obscure as written; the commentary's gloss "appearing as all colors" is followed. In "it pervades all embodied beings," the written འགེགས ("blocks") is read as འགེངས ("fills, pervades"), which fits the gloss of "connected." In "the intention being," the written དངོས་སོ is read as དགོངས་སོ. The variant root line plays on བུ "son" and བུ་ག "opening"; the commentary takes the "opening" in the sense of the vajra jewel, where the awakening mind is held. The commentary glosses torma (bali) as nectar that "makes the body strong," an etymology that plays on Sanskrit bala, strength. The phrase rendered "lets it swell out fully" (nam tshang 'phel ka bskyed) is obscure; the rendering is tentative. "Cow's udder" and "cow's hoof" are code terms for sites in the yogic body, the former apparently the hollow at the root of the uvula and the latter, as the text itself says, the passage from the eyebrows to the palate. The lemma is cited once as "relied on by the tongue" (bsten) and once as "shown by the tongue" (bstan); both are kept as they stand. The commentary explains "fingers" in the root verse as a word for the encircling lines rather than a measure. In the chapter colophon, "section" renders the Tibetan for a chapter division (Skt. paṭala). The "debt to the patron" refers to the common idea that a renunciant who eats donated food without proper practice incurs a debt to the donor; the yogin who eats in the way described incurs none. "Measure" renders the Tibetan for pramāṇa, which this commentary uses both for the bodily signs of each element and for a logical proof; the translation follows whichever sense the gloss gives. The list of actions reads "virtuous, nonvirtuous and 'drenma'"; the last word makes no sense as written and is taken as a slip for the similar word meaning "mixed." "King of the faculties" plays on the Sanskrit indriya, faculty, and Indra; the Tibetan is rendered in its sense here. "Binding white hair" denotes arresting or reversing the graying of the hair, a rejuvenation practice. "Hi ka" (the "one and a half letters") is read as the syllable hi followed by the consonant k, counted as half a letter. "The hūṃ of the ashes, as above" renders གོང་དུ་ཐལ་བའི་ཧཱུཾ; ཐལ་བ is taken as "ashes," matching the later stūpa visualization in which hūṃ arises from the light of the ashes of burnt things, but it could also be read as "the hūṃ projecting upward." "The golden door" (གསེར་གྱི་སྒོ) is translated literally; it seems to name a bodily aperture parallel to the "door of Brahmā," which the other opinion identifies with the crown. In "ཨཾ་ཧཾ་ཞེས་བྱ་བ་བའི་ས་བོན" the extra བ appears to be a copyist's doubling and is read simply as "are the seeds." "Cowherd's fruit" (བ་ལང་རྫིའི་འབྲས་བུ) is given literally, as I cannot securely identify the plant it names. The srang and zho are Tibetan units of weight, kept as such. The second explanation construes the syllable a of the root verse as "completely," so that the line speaks of complete bondage rather than of no bondage.
